Cross-Cultural Leadership English

Lead diverse teams with clear expectations, inclusive meetings, constructive feedback, and respectful disagreement.

For managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify scope, distinguish acknowledgment from approval, and confirm conditions.
  • Probe evidence, make a concession without surrendering the decision, and set a conduct boundary.
  • Define decision rights, invite recommendations, and delegate within explicit limits.
  • Offer bounded alternatives, use conditional commitments, and distinguish a forecast from a promise.
  • Manage turn-taking, interrupt constructively, paraphrase accurately, and close a hybrid meeting.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Ask about individual preferences and team norms. Nationality, accent, silence, or directness does not tell you what a person believes or how they will behave.

01Check meaning before assuming agreementWhen "fine" is not a green light+

Your goal

Clarify scope, distinguish acknowledgment from approval, and confirm conditions.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

When "fine" is not a green light

Aiko recorded agreement to a Monday product launch after hearing that the plan was "fine." Jordan then sent a staffing concern. The branch has six trained people; the launch plan needs eight. Two more people may finish training on Friday, but completion is not yet confirmed. Aiko can authorize the branch launch after the staffing check.

Aiko: International branch manager / Jordan: US-based operations lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which point is confirmed before the conversation?

Answer and explanation

B. Six people are trained.

The briefing confirms six trained people; the additional two are not yet confirmed.

2. Who can authorize the branch launch after the check?

Answer and explanation

C. Aiko

Aiko owns this decision. Jordan supplies the staffing information.

3. What needs clarification?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether "fine" approved the date or only the general plan

The immediate ambiguity is the scope of "fine." Scheduled training, acknowledged minutes and confirmation of wording do not establish readiness or transfer launch authority.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Acknowledgment confirms that a message was received or understood. Endorsement expresses support. Authorization permits action. Ask which of these the speaker is giving; do not record one as another.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
acknowledgment
A signal that a message has been received or understood.acknowledge receipt
endorsement
An expression of support for a proposal or position.endorse a proposal
authorization
Permission from someone entitled to approve an action.obtain authorization
reservation
A concern that limits or qualifies support.express a reservation
provisional
Not final; subject to a stated check or decision.provisional launch date
dependency
Something that must happen for another action to proceed.resolve a dependency
read-back
A spoken restatement used to check the shared record.give a read-back
sign-off
A formal or agreed approval step.get final sign-off
scope
The limits of what a statement or agreement covers.clarify the scope
conditional agreement
Support that applies only if a requirement is met.record conditional agreement
go/no-go decision
A decision to proceed or stop at a defined checkpoint.make a go/no-go decision
green light
Informal permission to proceed, not merely positive feedback.give the green light
clarification
Additional detail that resolves an unclear meaning.seek clarification
alignment
A shared understanding or compatible direction; not necessarily formal approval.check alignment on the date
pending
Waiting for an event, result, or decision.approval pending review
prerequisite
A requirement that must be met before another action.satisfy a prerequisite
assumption
Something treated as true before it has been established.test an assumption
explicit
Stated directly rather than left for the listener to infer.obtain explicit approval
implicit
Suggested or understood without being stated directly.avoid relying on implicit consent
qualification
A condition or limitation attached to a statement.add a qualification
tentative
Not firmly settled and still open to change.set a tentative date
supersede
Replace an earlier record, instruction, or decision.supersede the earlier minutes
discrepancy
A difference between accounts or records that should match.resolve a discrepancy
in principle
At the level of the general idea, with details still unresolved.agree in principle

"We agree in principle" supports the general idea. "We have authorized the launch" permits an action. A tentative date and a pending decision identify what remains unsettled.

C Notice the language

Acknowledgment is not authorization

A nod, "fine," or "I see" can acknowledge a message without approving the proposed action. This ambiguity is not unique to any nationality. A manager who hears agreement can check it without embarrassing the speaker: identify the precise decision, name any condition, and read back the record. Do not ask whether the colleague is "really committed" when the unresolved issue is staffing.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Specify the reference
When you say "fine," are you referring to the date or the overall approach?
Separate two meanings
I heard support for the approach, not approval of the date.
State the condition
My support is conditional on having eight trained people.
Check a commitment
Can I record that as a commitment, or is it still a possibility?
Preserve uncertainty
That remains provisional until we complete the staffing check.
Make an indirect question
Could you confirm whether the additional training is complete?
Read back the agreement
Let me read that back so we have the same decision record.
Close the loop
I will send the revised record and ask you to confirm it.
Expose an assumption
I may be reading more into that than you intended.
Limit general agreement
We agree in principle; the staffing condition is still open.
Check the reference
By "ready," do you mean trained, scheduled, or available for the first shift?
Separate response and decision
Please acknowledge receipt separately from confirming approval.
Identify the missing evidence
What evidence would let us change the status from pending to confirmed?
Qualify a date
Monday is our working date, subject to the Friday staffing check.
Replace an inaccurate record
This note supersedes the earlier statement that the date was final.
Check the whole condition
Is there any condition missing from that summary?
6 language and precision notes
Embedded questions
Use "Could you confirm whether the training is complete?" The clause after "whether" has statement order, not "whether is the training complete."
Meaning of "not yet"
"Not yet approved" leaves approval open. "Not approved" can sound like a refusal unless the context makes the pending status clear.
Precision before politeness
"Are we aligned?" is broad. "Are you approving Monday, provided that eight people are trained?" checks an action and its condition.
Name the reference
Replace a vague "that" with the relevant noun: "Do you support the sequence, the date, or both?"
Keep the qualification attached
"We support Monday, provided that eight people are trained" keeps the condition in the same sentence as the support.
Replace rather than add
"This note supersedes the earlier minutes" identifies which record now applies. "Here is another note" leaves that uncertain.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Choose the question that checks authorization, not receipt.

Answer and explanation

C. Have you authorized the Monday launch?

Authorization is permission to act; receiving or reading the plan does not provide it.

2. Complete: "Could you clarify whether ___?"

Answer and explanation

B. the date is final

An embedded question uses subject + verb: "the date is final."

3. Choose the record supported by the briefing.

Answer and explanation

D. Monday is provisional pending a staffing check.

The final staffing level is unknown, so the record must preserve the condition.

4. Which collocation means to settle an outstanding requirement?

Answer and explanation

A. resolve a dependency

A dependency is resolved when the required condition or preceding work is settled.

E Conversations

When "fine" is not a green light

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Aiko: International branch manager / Jordan: US-based operations lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • unconfirmed
  • flag
  • reservation
  • still provisional
  • green light
  • acknowledgment
  • read-back
  • certify
  • conditional
  • authorization
  1. Aiko
    Can we revisit Monday? I wrote it down as agreed, but your email says staffing is still an issue. Have I misunderstood you?
  2. Jordan
    You did. I was comfortable with the sequence, but I was not giving the 1 for Monday. We still have two people waiting to finish training.
  3. Aiko
    All right. When you said "fine," did you mean the sequence was workable, while the date still depended on the training?
  4. Jordan
    Exactly. My support was 2. Six people are trained now; we need eight. I should have stated the condition in the meeting.
  5. Aiko
    Right, I moved too quickly there. Have the two remaining people completed the final assessment?
  6. Jordan
    Not yet. Friday is the scheduled assessment, not a confirmed completion date. Until the results are in, the extra capacity is 3.
  7. Aiko
    Then I will correct the record. We support the launch sequence, but Monday is not authorized. Is there another constraint that I have missed?
  8. Jordan
    Only the handover. A trained supervisor must cover the first shift. I can check the roster, but I cannot 4 the training results myself.
  9. Aiko
    Understood. Please check the supervisor coverage and send me the assessment result by 3 p.m. branch time on Friday. Can you commit to that update, even if a result is missing?
  10. Jordan
    Yes. I can commit to an update, not a passing result. If either result is missing, I will 5 it rather than leave the field blank.
  11. Aiko
    Then I will mark the assessments pending. We can use confirmed when the results arrive, or blocked if something prevents the launch.
  12. Jordan
    That works. Could we also separate 6 from approval in the minutes? People sometimes say "understood" just to show they have heard the instruction.
  13. Aiko
    Yes. I will ask the decision owner to state approval explicitly. In this case that is me, after I review your staffing check.
  14. Jordan
    And if I say I have a 7, please keep the decision open long enough for me to explain it. I do not mean that I reject the whole plan.
  15. Aiko
    Agreed. One last check: if both assessments pass and the supervisor is available, you support Monday. If either condition fails, we discuss a new date. Correct?
  16. Jordan
    Correct. That is an accurate 8. It captures the conditions without turning them into guarantees.
  17. Aiko
    I will amend the minutes now and label Monday provisional. We will make the go/no-go decision after your Friday update, not before it.
  18. Jordan
    Please send me the revised paragraph. I can confirm its accuracy today; that confirmation should not be treated as 9 to launch.
  19. Aiko
    I will make that distinction explicit. Thank you for raising it before the team acted on the old record.
  20. Jordan
    Thanks for checking. Next time I will say, "The sequence works; the date is 10." That leaves less room for a different interpretation.

Answers and explanations
  1. green light Turn 2. Giving the green light means allowing an action to proceed. The printed "the" completes the phrase. Jordan supported the sequence but did not approve Monday.
  2. conditional Turn 4. Support depends on having eight trained people. Jordan accepts the approach only if that condition is met.
  3. unconfirmed Turn 6. A scheduled assessment does not establish extra capacity. Its outcome remains unknown.
  4. certify Turn 8. Jordan can obtain or discuss results but cannot formally attest to them. The sentence limits authority, not communication.
  5. flag Turn 10. To flag an item is to draw attention to it. Jordan cannot approve or resolve a missing result, and should not minimize it.
  6. acknowledgment Turn 12. "Understood" can acknowledge a message without approving it. The minutes need to preserve that distinction.
  7. reservation Turn 14. Having a reservation means having a concern or qualification. It need not mean rejecting the whole plan.
  8. read-back Turn 16. Aiko restates the agreement for confirmation. She is not predicting an outcome, referring a dispute upward, or removing a requirement.
  9. authorization Turn 18. Authorization is permission to act. Jordan can check the wording; only Aiko can authorize the launch after the staffing check.
  10. still provisional Turn 20. The date remains conditional. None of the evidence makes it final, irrelevant, or guaranteed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Aiko: Can we revisit Monday? I wrote it down as agreed, but your email says staffing is still an issue. Have I misunderstood you?
  2. Jordan: You did. I was comfortable with the sequence, but I was not giving the green light for Monday. We still have two people waiting to finish training.
  3. Aiko: All right. When you said "fine," did you mean the sequence was workable, while the date still depended on the training?
  4. Jordan: Exactly. My support was conditional. Six people are trained now; we need eight. I should have stated the condition in the meeting.
  5. Aiko: Right, I moved too quickly there. Have the two remaining people completed the final assessment?
  6. Jordan: Not yet. Friday is the scheduled assessment, not a confirmed completion date. Until the results are in, the extra capacity is unconfirmed.
  7. Aiko: Then I will correct the record. We support the launch sequence, but Monday is not authorized. Is there another constraint that I have missed?
  8. Jordan: Only the handover. A trained supervisor must cover the first shift. I can check the roster, but I cannot certify the training results myself.
  9. Aiko: Understood. Please check the supervisor coverage and send me the assessment result by 3 p.m. branch time on Friday. Can you commit to that update, even if a result is missing?
  10. Jordan: Yes. I can commit to an update, not a passing result. If either result is missing, I will flag it rather than leave the field blank.
  11. Aiko: Then I will mark the assessments pending. We can use confirmed when the results arrive, or blocked if something prevents the launch.
  12. Jordan: That works. Could we also separate acknowledgment from approval in the minutes? People sometimes say "understood" just to show they have heard the instruction.
  13. Aiko: Yes. I will ask the decision owner to state approval explicitly. In this case that is me, after I review your staffing check.
  14. Jordan: And if I say I have a reservation, please keep the decision open long enough for me to explain it. I do not mean that I reject the whole plan.
  15. Aiko: Agreed. One last check: if both assessments pass and the supervisor is available, you support Monday. If either condition fails, we discuss a new date. Correct?
  16. Jordan: Correct. That is an accurate read-back. It captures the conditions without turning them into guarantees.
  17. Aiko: I will amend the minutes now and label Monday provisional. We will make the go/no-go decision after your Friday update, not before it.
  18. Jordan: Please send me the revised paragraph. I can confirm its accuracy today; that confirmation should not be treated as authorization to launch.
  19. Aiko: I will make that distinction explicit. Thank you for raising it before the team acted on the old record.
  20. Jordan: Thanks for checking. Next time I will say, "The sequence works; the date is still provisional." That leaves less room for a different interpretation.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Reopening the launch agreement

The branch manager follows up on the lesson's launch meeting after receiving a staffing concern.

  1. Branch manager: I recorded agreement when you said the plan was fine. Your email suggests a staffing concern.
  2. Operations lead: I meant the launch sequence looked reasonable, not that we had enough people.
  3. Branch manager: Thank you for the clarification. Which part of my recorded expectation needs correcting?
  4. Operations lead: Please separate support for the sequence from confirmation that staffing is sufficient.
  5. Branch manager: Was your staffing concern already resolved when we finished the meeting?
  6. Operations lead: No. My email raises it because we still need to discuss coverage.
  7. Branch manager: Then my assumption was too broad. I should not have recorded full agreement.
  8. Operations lead: Could we reopen that point before presenting the plan as settled?
  9. Branch manager: Yes. I'll correct the record and invite both colleagues to clarify their positions.
  10. Operations lead: That works. We can discuss coverage without implying that either colleague approved it.
Available does not mean assigned

A separate fictional branch case: a training coordinator has two free hours Thursday, but no assignment has been agreed.

  1. Branch manager: You said you were available Thursday. May I announce you as the workshop facilitator?
  2. Training coordinator: Could you clarify the assignment first? I have two hours free, not an unlimited opening.
  3. Branch manager: I was assuming availability meant a commitment to lead the whole session.
  4. Training coordinator: That wasn't my intention. I need the session length and preparation expectation.
  5. Branch manager: We haven't settled either detail. What can I accurately tell the planning group?
  6. Training coordinator: Say that I have a possible two-hour slot, subject to reviewing the work.
  7. Branch manager: So you haven't accepted facilitation or preparation duties at this stage?
  8. Training coordinator: Correct. Please ask the group to define those duties before requesting confirmation.
  9. Branch manager: I'll bring back a specific request instead of adding your name to the agenda.
  10. Training coordinator: Thanks. Once the scope is clear, I can give you a definite answer.
Whose end of day?

A separate fictional handoff connects offices using Eastern and Pacific time. A request says only 'end of day.'

  1. Branch coordinator: Your request says end of day. Which time zone should we use for this handoff?
  2. Headquarters analyst: I meant Eastern time, but I didn't include a specific hour.
  3. Branch coordinator: Our team uses Pacific time. That leaves room for a different assumption.
  4. Headquarters analyst: You're right. I also need to clarify whether I want preliminary or reviewed numbers.
  5. Branch coordinator: Which version does your next meeting actually require us to supply?
  6. Headquarters analyst: I need reviewed numbers, but I haven't checked when your review finishes.
  7. Branch coordinator: Then let's establish availability before turning that expectation into a deadline.
  8. Headquarters analyst: Could you check the review schedule while I confirm the meeting's cutoff?
  9. Branch coordinator: Yes. We'll agree an exact time and zone after those two checks.
  10. Headquarters analyst: I'll leave the delivery time unconfirmed until we have that clarification.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

When "fine" is not a green light
  1. Read turns 1-10 with a partner; insert your selected answers without adding facts.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after each clarification question until the partner finishes the printed reply.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A vendor demonstration, not a purchase

Aiko and Jordan now discuss a software demonstration. The team likes the interface. Security review is pending, and the purchasing director alone can approve a contract.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Aiko: "The team likes the interface. May I record that as ___?"

Answer and explanation

B. positive feedback on the demonstration

The team evaluated a demonstration; neither review nor purchase is complete.

2. Jordan: "Yes, but the purchase remains ___ security review."

Answer and explanation

B. subject to

"Subject to" makes review a condition, not something to bypass.

3. Aiko: "Who has ___ for the contract?"

Answer and explanation

D. approval authority

The question asks who is entitled to approve the purchase.

4. Jordan: "The purchasing director. Our positive feedback does not ___ that approval."

Answer and explanation

A. constitute

"Constitute" means count as. Feedback may precede approval, but does not itself amount to approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cross-Cultural Leadership English
Professional audience: managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators
Scope: Ask about individual preferences and team norms. Nationality, accent, silence, or directness does not tell you what a person believes or how they will behave.
Lesson 01: Check meaning before assuming agreement
Original fictional case: Aiko recorded agreement to a Monday product launch after hearing that the plan was "fine." Jordan then sent a staffing concern. The branch has six trained people; the launch plan needs eight. Two more people may finish training on Friday, but completion is not yet confirmed. Aiko can authorize the branch launch after the staffing check.
Language workshop: When "fine" is not a green light
Communication goal: Clarify scope, distinguish acknowledgment from approval, and confirm conditions.
Language guidance: Acknowledgment confirms that a message was received or understood. Endorsement expresses support. Authorization permits action. Ask which of these the speaker is giving; do not record one as another.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say "fine," are you referring to the date or the overall approach? / I heard support for the approach, not approval of the date. / My support is conditional on having eight trained people. / Can I record that as a commitment, or is it still a possibility? / That remains provisional until we complete the staffing check. / Could you confirm whether the additional training is complete? / Let me read that back so we have the same decision record. / I will send the revised record and ask you to confirm it. / I may be reading more into that than you intended. / We agree in principle; the staffing condition is still open. / By "ready," do you mean trained, scheduled, or available for the first shift? / Please acknowledge receipt separately from confirming approval. / What evidence would let us change the status from pending to confirmed? / Monday is our working date, subject to the Friday staffing check. / This note supersedes the earlier statement that the date was final. / Is there any condition missing from that summary?
Vocabulary: acknowledgment: A signal that a message has been received or understood. / endorsement: An expression of support for a proposal or position. / authorization: Permission from someone entitled to approve an action. / reservation: A concern that limits or qualifies support. / provisional: Not final; subject to a stated check or decision. / dependency: Something that must happen for another action to proceed. / read-back: A spoken restatement used to check the shared record. / sign-off: A formal or agreed approval step. / scope: The limits of what a statement or agreement covers. / conditional agreement: Support that applies only if a requirement is met. / go/no-go decision: A decision to proceed or stop at a defined checkpoint. / green light: Informal permission to proceed, not merely positive feedback. / clarification: Additional detail that resolves an unclear meaning. / alignment: A shared understanding or compatible direction; not necessarily formal approval. / pending: Waiting for an event, result, or decision. / prerequisite: A requirement that must be met before another action. / assumption: Something treated as true before it has been established. / explicit: Stated directly rather than left for the listener to infer. / implicit: Suggested or understood without being stated directly. / qualification: A condition or limitation attached to a statement. / tentative: Not firmly settled and still open to change. / supersede: Replace an earlier record, instruction, or decision. / discrepancy: A difference between accounts or records that should match. / in principle: At the level of the general idea, with details still unresolved.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with a partner; insert your selected answers without adding facts. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after each clarification question until the partner finishes the printed reply. Read the four-line vendor conversation twice, switching roles. Use the answer key only after both readers have selected their choices.
Partner: Aiko: International branch manager / Jordan: US-based operations lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Aiko and Jordan now discuss a software demonstration. The team likes the interface. Security review is pending, and the purchasing director alone can approve a contract.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Aiko: I will amend the minutes now and label Monday provisional. We will make the go/no-go decision after your Friday update, not before it.
Jordan: Please send me the revised paragraph. I can confirm its accuracy today; that confirmation should not be treated as authorization to launch.
Aiko: I will make that distinction explicit. Thank you for raising it before the team acted on the old record.
Jordan: Thanks for checking. Next time I will say, "The sequence works; the date is still provisional." That leaves less room for a different interpretation.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Clarify scope, distinguish acknowledgment from approval, and confirm conditions.
Language guidance: Acknowledgment confirms that a message was received or understood. Endorsement expresses support. Authorization permits action. Ask which of these the speaker is giving; do not record one as another.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say "fine," are you referring to the date or the overall approach? / I heard support for the approach, not approval of the date. / My support is conditional on having eight trained people. / Can I record that as a commitment, or is it still a possibility? / That remains provisional until we complete the staffing check. / Could you confirm whether the additional training is complete? / Let me read that back so we have the same decision record. / I will send the revised record and ask you to confirm it. / I may be reading more into that than you intended. / We agree in principle; the staffing condition is still open. / By "ready," do you mean trained, scheduled, or available for the first shift? / Please acknowledge receipt separately from confirming approval. / What evidence would let us change the status from pending to confirmed? / Monday is our working date, subject to the Friday staffing check. / This note supersedes the earlier statement that the date was final. / Is there any condition missing from that summary?
Vocabulary: acknowledgment: A signal that a message has been received or understood. / endorsement: An expression of support for a proposal or position. / authorization: Permission from someone entitled to approve an action. / reservation: A concern that limits or qualifies support. / provisional: Not final; subject to a stated check or decision. / dependency: Something that must happen for another action to proceed. / read-back: A spoken restatement used to check the shared record. / sign-off: A formal or agreed approval step. / scope: The limits of what a statement or agreement covers. / conditional agreement: Support that applies only if a requirement is met. / go/no-go decision: A decision to proceed or stop at a defined checkpoint. / green light: Informal permission to proceed, not merely positive feedback. / clarification: Additional detail that resolves an unclear meaning. / alignment: A shared understanding or compatible direction; not necessarily formal approval. / pending: Waiting for an event, result, or decision. / prerequisite: A requirement that must be met before another action. / assumption: Something treated as true before it has been established. / explicit: Stated directly rather than left for the listener to infer. / implicit: Suggested or understood without being stated directly. / qualification: A condition or limitation attached to a statement. / tentative: Not firmly settled and still open to change. / supersede: Replace an earlier record, instruction, or decision. / discrepancy: A difference between accounts or records that should match. / in principle: At the level of the general idea, with details still unresolved.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with a partner; insert your selected answers without adding facts. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause after each clarification question until the partner finishes the printed reply. Read the four-line vendor conversation twice, switching roles. Use the answer key only after both readers have selected their choices.
Partner: Aiko: International branch manager / Jordan: US-based operations lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Aiko and Jordan now discuss a software demonstration. The team likes the interface. Security review is pending, and the purchasing director alone can approve a contract.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Aiko: I will amend the minutes now and label Monday provisional. We will make the go/no-go decision after your Friday update, not before it.
Jordan: Please send me the revised paragraph. I can confirm its accuracy today; that confirmation should not be treated as authorization to launch.
Aiko: I will make that distinction explicit. Thank you for raising it before the team acted on the old record.
Jordan: Thanks for checking. Next time I will say, "The sequence works; the date is still provisional." That leaves less room for a different interpretation.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cross-Cultural Leadership English
Professional audience: managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators
Scope: Ask about individual preferences and team norms. Nationality, accent, silence, or directness does not tell you what a person believes or how they will behave.
Lesson 01: Check meaning before assuming agreement
Original fictional case: Aiko recorded agreement to a Monday product launch after hearing that the plan was "fine." Jordan then sent a staffing concern. The branch has six trained people; the launch plan needs eight. Two more people may finish training on Friday, but completion is not yet confirmed. Aiko can authorize the branch launch after the staffing check.

02Challenge ideas respectfullyA sharp objection at the budget review+

Your goal

Probe evidence, make a concession without surrendering the decision, and set a conduct boundary.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A sharp objection at the budget review

Mei proposes a six-week pilot with two account managers. Chris says, "This proposal makes no sense." The plan assumes two hours of follow-up per account each week; the last trial used four. The staffing allocation is fixed for this pilot. Mei can revise the account count, but cannot add staff without separate approval.

Mei: International branch manager / Chris: US-based commercial lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which assumption is contradicted by the previous trial?

Answer and explanation

C. Follow-up will take two hours.

The trial used four hours, which challenges the two-hour estimate.

2. Which adjustment is within Mei's stated authority?

Answer and explanation

B. Change the number of pilot accounts.

The briefing gives Mei discretion over account count, not additional staffing.

3. Which response separates the issue from the person?

Answer and explanation

A. Ask for the challenged assumption and supporting evidence.

A specific evidence request tests the proposal without inventing a personal motive.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A concession accepts a particular point, not necessarily the whole argument. A counterproposal offers a different course of action. Constructive pushback needs neither personal agreement nor personal criticism.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pushback
Resistance or a challenge to a request or proposal.offer constructive pushback
premise
A starting claim on which an argument depends.challenge the premise
stress-test
Examine a plan under difficult but plausible conditions.stress-test an assumption
counterproposal
A different proposal offered in response.put forward a counterproposal
evidence base
The information supporting a conclusion or estimate.strengthen the evidence base
concession
A point accepted without necessarily accepting the entire argument.make a limited concession
feasibility
Whether something can realistically be done.assess feasibility
capacity constraint
A limit on the work available people or systems can handle.work within a capacity constraint
non-negotiable
A requirement that cannot be traded away in this discussion.state a non-negotiable
personal remark
A comment about a person rather than the issue under review.avoid personal remarks
burden of proof
Responsibility for supplying evidence for a claim.meet the burden of proof
devil's advocate
Someone testing a position by arguing against it, sometimes without holding that view.play devil's advocate
rebuttal
A response that challenges an argument with reasons or evidence.offer an evidence-based rebuttal
rationale
The reasoning behind a choice or proposal.explain the rationale
substantiation
Evidence or reasoning that supports a claim.request substantiation
extrapolate
Extend a finding beyond the conditions or data directly observed.extrapolate from a small trial
directional evidence
Information suggesting a tendency without establishing a precise result.treat the result as directional evidence
false dichotomy
Presenting two alternatives as the only choices when others exist.challenge a false dichotomy
point of contention
The particular issue on which people disagree.isolate the point of contention
constructive dissent
Reasoned disagreement intended to improve a decision.invite constructive dissent
ad hominem
Directed at the person instead of addressing the argument.avoid ad hominem attacks
red line
An informal term for a limit that must not be crossed.state a clear red line
viable alternative
Another course of action that can realistically work.offer a viable alternative
common ground
A purpose or position shared by people who disagree.establish common ground

A rebuttal challenges the reasoning; an ad hominem attack targets the person. Directional evidence can justify further testing without substantiating a full rollout. State exactly how far the evidence supports the claim.

C Notice the language

Test the claim; address the delivery

Some colleagues use debate to test a proposal rather than signal personal dislike. That possibility does not excuse contempt. Ask which assumption is challenged and what evidence would change the conclusion. A claim about the plan is different from a judgment about your competence. If the speaker insults people or repeatedly talks over them, name the behavior and set a limit; you do not need to prove hostile intent before doing so.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask for the premise
Which assumption are you challenging?
Request evidence
What are you basing that estimate on?
Concede one point
I accept the capacity concern; I do not accept that the whole pilot is unworkable.
Name the distinction
Let us separate the objective from the proposed method.
Set a boundary
Challenge the proposal, please, not the competence of the people presenting it.
Invite an alternative
What would a workable version look like within the same staffing limit?
Limit certainty
That evidence supports a smaller pilot, not a guaranteed failure.
Close firmly
We will revise the account count and test the workload before expanding.
Narrow the disagreement
Our point of contention is the time estimate, not the need for the pilot.
Check comparability
Which conditions in the earlier trial match the conditions we are proposing?
Test a broad claim
What would have to be true for that conclusion to hold?
Name a shared aim
We both want a pilot that produces useful evidence without overloading the team.
Reject a false choice
Our choices are not limited to the current plan or canceling the pilot.
Invite a rebuttal
Which part of that calculation would you dispute?
Limit extrapolation
We should not extrapolate to a full rollout from this sample alone.
Resume after a boundary
Now that we have addressed the remark, let us compare the two options.
6 language and precision notes
Concession + contrast
Use "I accept X; however, Y remains unresolved." Acceptance of one point is not agreement with the whole conclusion.
Calibrated certainty
"The trial suggests" is narrower than "The trial proves." Match the strength of the verb to the evidence available.
Boundary + invitation
Combine a limit with a route back to the work: "No personal remarks. Please show us the workload calculation."
Challenge a claim, not a motive
"That estimate needs substantiation" requests evidence. "You are trying to mislead us" assigns a motive that the figures alone do not establish.
Find a third option
"Either we launch at full scale or cancel" excludes a smaller trial. "Could we reduce the scope?" reopens that option.
Different levels of firmness
"The sample may be too small" allows uncertainty about evidence. "Please stop the personal remarks" states a conduct boundary directly.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Choose the response that concedes a fact but preserves a narrower proposal.

Answer and explanation

B. I accept the four-hour figure; a smaller pilot may still fit.

The response accepts the evidence while leaving the account count open to revision.

2. Complete: "The previous trial ___ a workload risk; it does not prove failure."

Answer and explanation

D. indicates

"Indicates" signals evidence of risk without claiming certainty.

3. Which sentence sets a conduct boundary without rejecting criticism?

Answer and explanation

C. Please keep the comments on the proposal, not the people.

It protects people while allowing substantive disagreement.

4. Which question asks for a counterproposal within the constraint?

Answer and explanation

A. How many accounts can two managers support?

Asking how many accounts two managers can support seeks an option within the fixed staffing allocation. The alternatives assume unsupported improvement, add unauthorized capacity, or overstate the earlier evidence.

A sharp objection at the budget review

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: International branch manager / Chris: US-based commercial lead

    The follow-up time. Your sheet allows two hours per account each week. The last trial took four, and it had fewer handoffs than this one.
  4. Mei

    Please keep this on the workload, not the team's competence. I welcome 4, but personal judgments will not help us verify the estimate.
  9. Chris
    Fair point. That was too personal. My concern is the estimate. I am trying to prevent an overloaded team, not undermine you.
  10. Mei
    I hear you. But the 5 on the team still matters. Please correct that remark, and then let us work through your alternative.
  11. Chris
    Within two managers, I would cut the account count in half. We could then measure actual follow-up time before asking for a larger allocation.
  12. Mei
    That is a useful 6. Keeping the staffing fixed matters, because additional headcount would need a different approval.
  13. Chris

  14. Mei
    We need to test that. A smaller sample may give directional evidence, but it will not 7 a full rollout. We will state that limitation in the review.
  15. Chris

  19. Chris

Answers and explanations
  1. premise Turn 2. A premise is a starting assumption. Mei asks which assumption in the plan Chris is challenging.
  2. evidence gap Turn 4. The estimate lacks supporting evidence. Neither approval nor extra staffing has been established, and motives remain unknown.
  3. concede Turn 6. Mei accepts the template point. She does not reject, ignore, or delay that acknowledgment.
  4. scrutiny Turn 8. Scrutiny means close examination of the work. Mei welcomes that examination while setting a boundary around personal judgments.
  5. impact Turn 10. The focus is how the wording affected others, not a legal judgment or a vote on the wording.
  6. counterproposal Turn 12. Chris offers an alternative design. He neither grants approval nor demands unconditional compliance.
  7. validate Turn 14. The smaller sample cannot establish that a full rollout is justified. It may precede or inform a later decision.
  8. non-negotiable Turn 16. Two managers is a fixed constraint for this decision. The account count and review points can still change.
  9. retain Turn 18. The team can keep a substantive disagreement while correcting the personal remark. It need not hide or resolve the design issue immediately.
  10. stress-test Turn 20. The revised pilot will examine the assumption under real workload. Announcing an estimate does not test it.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Chris: This proposal makes no sense. Two account managers cannot run a pilot on that scale and keep their normal accounts. We will burn through the team before week two.
  2. Mei: I want to understand the objection. Which premise are you challenging: the number of accounts, the follow-up time, or the length of the pilot?
  3. Chris: The follow-up time. Your sheet allows two hours per account each week. The last trial took four, and it had fewer handoffs than this one.
  4. Mei: Then we have a specific evidence gap, not just a general concern. Can you show us the trial log and explain what is comparable?
  5. Chris: Yes. Here are the entries. Most of the time went into resolving customer questions, not administration. A new template will not remove those conversations.
  6. Mei: I concede that point. We should not assume the template halves the workload. I am not yet persuaded that we must abandon the pilot.
  7. Chris: If you want my honest view, the team that wrote this has not understood the customers. That is why the estimate is so far off.
  8. Mei: Please keep this on the workload, not the team's competence. I welcome scrutiny, but personal judgments will not help us verify the estimate.
  9. Chris: Fair point. That was too personal. My concern is the estimate. I am trying to prevent an overloaded team, not undermine you.
  10. Mei: I hear you. But the impact on the team still matters. Please correct that remark, and then let us work through your alternative.
  11. Chris: Within two managers, I would cut the account count in half. We could then measure actual follow-up time before asking for a larger allocation.
  12. Mei: That is a useful counterproposal. Keeping the staffing fixed matters, because additional headcount would need a different approval.
  13. Chris: Wait, would half the accounts tell us enough? We still need a useful pilot, not just a manageable one.
  14. Mei: We need to test that. A smaller sample may give directional evidence, but it will not validate a full rollout. We will state that limitation in the review.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Turning a dismissal into an objection

A separate fictional branch proposal allocates one employee to two simultaneous reception desks. The facilitator addresses a department lead's broad dismissal of that proposal.

  1. Meeting facilitator: You said this proposal makes no sense. Which specific assumption are you objecting to?
  2. Department lead: It assigns one employee to two reception desks operating at the same time.
  3. Meeting facilitator: That's a concrete concern. Please explain the coverage problem without dismissing the people who drafted it.
  4. Department lead: One person cannot cover both desks simultaneously. Are you asking me to soften that objection?
  5. Meeting facilitator: No. Keep the objection direct; the boundary concerns personal criticism, not disagreement about staffing.
  6. Department lead: Then we need either another employee or different operating times for the desks.
  7. Meeting facilitator: Additional staffing isn't confirmed. Could we assess staggered hours before assuming that resource is available?
  8. Department lead: Yes, but we'd need to check whether staggered hours meet the reception requirements.
  9. Meeting facilitator: I'll ask the proposal owner to assess those options with the requirement still visible.
  10. Department lead: Please record simultaneous coverage as unresolved, not as an approved staffing arrangement.
Protecting a review step

A separate fictional branch plans a customer mailing. The proposed schedule omits a review step agreed by this project team.

  1. Campaign lead: Let's remove the review meeting so the mailing can go out sooner.
  2. Branch manager: I understand the timing goal. My objection is that our agreed review would disappear.
  3. Campaign lead: Do you think the team is incapable of producing a reliable mailing?
  4. Branch manager: I'm not questioning anyone's ability. I'm pointing to a specific missing step.
  5. Campaign lead: Could we review only the changed sections instead of meeting about every page?
  6. Branch manager: That may work, provided the reviewer agrees the narrower scope covers the changes.
  7. Campaign lead: So your boundary concerns releasing unreviewed changes, not keeping a meeting for its own sake?
  8. Branch manager: Yes. Let's ask the reviewer what they need before confirming a shorter process.
  9. Campaign lead: I'll bring the changed sections and request a focused review proposal.
  10. Branch manager: Then we can discuss the date with that condition visible, rather than assume approval.
Responding to an interruption complaint

A separate fictional branch plan schedules training Monday, but its required practice accounts will be available Wednesday. The project lead interrupted the scheduler twice while this dependency was being explained.

  1. Scheduler: You interrupted me twice while I explained the dependency. Could I finish the timing concern?
  2. Project lead: Yes. I thought the dates were settled, but please explain what the proposal overlooks.
  3. Scheduler: Training is Monday, while the required practice accounts won't be available until Wednesday.
  4. Project lead: Could the trainer demonstrate the process Monday and let participants practice afterward?
  5. Scheduler: That changes the session scope. The planned Monday session requires participants to use those accounts.
  6. Project lead: Then my proposal assumes access before it's available. We should assess moving the session.
  7. Scheduler: Yes. Please let me complete these explanations before answering; otherwise the dependency gets lost.
  8. Project lead: That's a fair boundary. Could you check a later session with the trainer?
  9. Scheduler: I'll ask, but I can't confirm their availability or a revised date yet.
  10. Project lead: Understood. We'll record the Monday conflict and keep the replacement date pending that check.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A sharp objection at the budget review
  1. Read turns 1-10 once with a neutral tone and once with firm emphasis on "workload" and "competence." Keep the words unchanged.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause at each concession or boundary; the listener repeats that exact phrase before continuing.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Challenge a sales forecast

A forecast assumes ten renewals. Only seven customers have confirmed. You can revise the forecast, but you cannot promise the other three renewals.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Chris: "The forecast is optimistic. Which ___ support ten renewals?"

Answer and explanation

B. confirmations

A claim of confirmed renewals needs customer confirmations.

2. Mei: "I ___ seven confirmed renewals, not ten."

Answer and explanation

D. can substantiate

"Substantiate" means support with evidence; seven is the supported figure.

3. Chris: "Then label the other three as ___, not committed."

Answer and explanation

C. possible

The three renewals are possibilities, not established commitments.

4. Mei: "Agreed. We will challenge the ___, not the forecaster's character."

Answer and explanation

C. assumption

The disagreement concerns the estimate and its basis, not a personal characteristic.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

Professional audience: managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators

Lesson 02: Challenge ideas respectfully
Original fictional case: Mei proposes a six-week pilot with two account managers. Chris says, "This proposal makes no sense." The plan assumes two hours of follow-up per account each week; the last trial used four. The staffing allocation is fixed for this pilot. Mei can revise the account count, but cannot add staff without separate approval.
Language workshop: A sharp objection at the budget review
Communication goal: Probe evidence, make a concession without surrendering the decision, and set a conduct boundary.
Language guidance: A concession accepts a particular point, not necessarily the whole argument. A counterproposal offers a different course of action. Constructive pushback needs neither personal agreement nor personal criticism.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which assumption are you challenging? / What are you basing that estimate on? / I accept the capacity concern; I do not accept that the whole pilot is unworkable. / Let us separate the objective from the proposed method. / Challenge the proposal, please, not the competence of the people presenting it. / What would a workable version look like within the same staffing limit? / That evidence supports a smaller pilot, not a guaranteed failure. / We will revise the account count and test the workload before expanding. / Our point of contention is the time estimate, not the need for the pilot. / Which conditions in the earlier trial match the conditions we are proposing? / What would have to be true for that conclusion to hold? / We both want a pilot that produces useful evidence without overloading the team. / Our choices are not limited to the current plan or canceling the pilot. / Which part of that calculation would you dispute? / We should not extrapolate to a full rollout from this sample alone. / Now that we have addressed the remark, let us compare the two options.
Vocabulary: pushback: Resistance or a challenge to a request or proposal. / premise: A starting claim on which an argument depends. / stress-test: Examine a plan under difficult but plausible conditions. / counterproposal: A different proposal offered in response. / evidence base: The information supporting a conclusion or estimate. / concession: A point accepted without necessarily accepting the entire argument. / feasibility: Whether something can realistically be done. / capacity constraint: A limit on the work available people or systems can handle. / non-negotiable: A requirement that cannot be traded away in this discussion. / personal remark: A comment about a person rather than the issue under review. / burden of proof: Responsibility for supplying evidence for a claim. / devil's advocate: Someone testing a position by arguing against it, sometimes without holding that view. / rebuttal: A response that challenges an argument with reasons or evidence. / rationale: The reasoning behind a choice or proposal. / substantiation: Evidence or reasoning that supports a claim. / extrapolate: Extend a finding beyond the conditions or data directly observed. / directional evidence: Information suggesting a tendency without establishing a precise result. / false dichotomy: Presenting two alternatives as the only choices when others exist. / point of contention: The particular issue on which people disagree. / constructive dissent: Reasoned disagreement intended to improve a decision. / ad hominem: Directed at the person instead of addressing the argument. / red line: An informal term for a limit that must not be crossed. / viable alternative: Another course of action that can realistically work. / common ground: A purpose or position shared by people who disagree.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 once with a neutral tone and once with firm emphasis on "workload" and "competence." Keep the words unchanged. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Pause at each concession or boundary; the listener repeats that exact phrase before continuing. Complete the forecast conversation, then read it without substituting "confirmed" for "possible."
Partner: Mei: International branch manager / Chris: US-based commercial lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A forecast assumes ten renewals. Only seven customers have confirmed. You can revise the forecast, but you cannot promise the other three renewals.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Chris: I will work with operations on the revised count. I also owe the team a correction for the remark about their understanding of customers.
Mei: Please make that correction in the same group. We can retain the disagreement about the design without leaving the personal remark unaddressed.
Chris: I will. The estimate needs work; I had no basis for questioning the team. I will say that before we circulate the revision.
Mei: Good. Our decision is to stress-test the workload assumption with a smaller pilot, not to treat either of today's estimates as a guarantee.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
