03Make authority and participation clearDelegating without disappearing+

Your goal

Define decision rights, invite recommendations, and delegate within explicit limits.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Delegating without disappearing

Sofia has been approving every small change to an onboarding process. Alex wants the team to act faster but does not know where its authority ends. For this fictional project, Sofia can delegate changes to internal task order. Customer promises and additional spending still require her approval. A process trial will run for two weeks.

Sofia: International customer-service manager / Alex: US-based team lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which decision can Sofia delegate in this case?

Answer and explanation

C. Internal task order

Only internal task order is within the stated delegation.

2. What remains reserved to Sofia?

Answer and explanation

B. Customer promises and extra spending

The briefing identifies these two approval boundaries.

3. How long will the process trial run?

Answer and explanation

D. Two weeks

The trial has a defined two-week period, after which the process can be reviewed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Consultation asks for input; consensus requires shared agreement. Delegation grants specified authority, not unlimited discretion. State the remit and escalation threshold before asking someone to take ownership.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
decision rights
Who may recommend, approve, or decide a particular matter.clarify decision rights
delegation
Giving another person defined responsibility or authority.delegate a decision
discretion
Freedom to choose within stated limits.exercise discretion
guardrail
A limit intended to keep a decision within an acceptable range.set clear guardrails
escalation threshold
The point at which an issue must go to another authority.agree an escalation threshold
decision owner
The person accountable for making a particular decision.name the decision owner
consultation
Seeking input before an action or decision.hold a consultation
consensus
Broad agreement, which must not be inferred from silence alone.build consensus
accountability
Responsibility for explaining actions and following through.retain accountability
remit
The area of work or authority assigned to a role.stay within your remit
exception
A case that falls outside the normal rule or arrangement.approve an exception
RACI
A role map: Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, Informed. Organizations may apply it differently.use a RACI matrix
mandate
An assigned purpose and authority to carry out work.define the team's mandate
autonomy
Freedom to organize or decide work within agreed limits.grant operational autonomy
approval boundary
The point beyond which a decision needs another person's permission.respect the approval boundary
retained authority
Decision power that is not passed to the person doing the work.specify retained authority
decision gate
A checkpoint at which a named owner decides whether work may proceed.pass a decision gate
span of control
The number of people or teams directly supervised by one manager.review a manager's span of control
separation of duties
Dividing important tasks among roles to reduce conflicts or unchecked control.maintain separation of duties
oversight
Monitoring and review by someone responsible for the work.retain management oversight
accountable owner
The person answerable for a specified result or decision.name an accountable owner
approval matrix
A table showing who can approve which decisions and limits.check the approval matrix
revocable
Able to be withdrawn by the person or body granting it.grant revocable authority
informed stakeholder
Someone who needs updates but does not necessarily decide.keep an informed stakeholder updated

Autonomy describes room to act; oversight describes how the work is monitored. They can coexist. An informed stakeholder receives updates, a consulted colleague supplies input, and an approver grants permission.

C Notice the language

Consultation is not a vote

A manager can invite disagreement and still own the final decision. Conversely, an invitation to "take ownership" does not tell a team which decisions it can make. The missing information is authority, not confidence. Specify what is delegated, what needs consultation, and what remains reserved. Do not equate an employee's request for clarity with reluctance to lead, or a confident suggestion with a claim to unlimited authority.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Invite a recommendation
Bring me your preferred option and the reason for it.
Delegate clearly
You may change the internal sequence without seeking approval each time.
Reserve a decision
Customer commitments remain my decision.
Set a limit
That discretion does not include additional spending.
Define an escalation
If the change affects a customer promise, bring it back to me.
Distinguish roles
I need your input; I am not asking you to make the final decision.
Prevent silent consent
I will ask each owner to confirm the responsibilities assigned to them.
Schedule review
We will review the trial after two weeks using the agreed criteria.
Define autonomy
Within the agreed limits, the task order is yours to decide.
Keep oversight explicit
I am delegating the sequence, not stepping away from oversight.
Check a boundary
Does this change fall within the authority you have delegated to me?
Distinguish recommendation and action
You may recommend the tool, but purchasing it still requires approval.
Name the accountable owner
Let us name one person who is answerable for the trial result.
Require timely escalation
Raise the issue before making the customer commitment, not afterward.
Explain the checkpoint
The two-week review is a decision gate, not automatic permission to continue.
Confirm understanding
Please state which decisions you can make and which you will bring back to me.
6 language and precision notes
Permission and obligation
"You may" grants permission. "You must" states a requirement. "You can" may describe either ability or permission; make the context explicit.
Within and beyond
"Within your remit" identifies permitted scope. "Beyond your remit" signals a need for a different owner or approval.
Consult versus approve
"Consult finance" requires input; it does not automatically give finance final approval authority. State the actual arrangement.
Name the actor
"Sofia approves additional spending" identifies the owner. "Spending must be approved" names a requirement but leaves the owner unclear.
Permission is not a requirement
"You may adjust the order" permits a change; "you must adjust the order" requires it. Keep this distinction when relaying instructions.
Separate advice from authority
"I recommend buying the tool" gives advice. "I authorize the purchase" grants permission only when the speaker holds that authority.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which instruction delegates authority with a clear boundary?

Answer and explanation

D. Reorder internal tasks; seek approval before changing customer promises.

It names both the delegated area and the reserved decision.

2. Complete: "You ___ notify me if a change affects a customer promise." The requirement is compulsory.

Answer and explanation

B. must

"Must" expresses the compulsory notification stated in the question.

3. Which statement describes consultation, not approval?

Answer and explanation

A. Finance supplies cost information before Sofia decides.

Supplying input is consultation; the other choices exercise decision authority.

4. The team disagrees after everyone has contributed. What is still possible?

Answer and explanation

C. Sofia can decide after considering the input.

Participation and disagreement do not remove Sofia's assigned decision rights. Neither consultation nor trial coordination expands Alex's spending authority.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Delegating without disappearing

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sofia: International customer-service manager / Alex: US-based team lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • accountability
  • consulted
  • decision rights
  • remit
  • delegated
  • escalation threshold
  • review
  • discretion
  • responsibilities
  • exception
  1. Alex
    The team wants more freedom on onboarding, but "take ownership" has not been clear enough. Yesterday we waited for your reply before moving a routine internal check.
  2. Sofia
    That is a fair criticism. I have asked for initiative without defining the 1. Let us separate what you can decide from what needs my approval.
  3. Alex
    Our recommendation is to move the internal document check earlier. It would not change what we promise customers or require additional spending.
  4. Sofia
    You have my permission to decide the internal task order. That is now 2 to you for the two-week trial; you do not need to ask about each change.
  5. Alex
    Does that include changing a customer's promised completion date if an internal delay makes the original date difficult?
  6. Sofia
    No. Customer promises are outside that 3. Bring those changes to me with the reason and the options before making a new commitment.
  7. Alex
    Understood. And if the team wants a paid document-checking tool, we should stop at the spending boundary rather than order it and tell you afterward.
  8. Sofia
    Exactly. Additional spending is an 4 that needs approval. The team can research the tool and recommend it, but not purchase it under this delegation.
  9. Alex
    I would like input from customer support before choosing the sequence. Do they need to agree, or is their role to identify problems we might miss?
  10. Sofia
    For the internal sequence, they are 5. You make the decision after using their input. Do not describe their silence as agreement.
  11. Alex
    Then I will send them two options and ask for risks by Thursday noon. If they need more time, they can say so rather than approve by default.
  12. Sofia
    Good. If a disagreement concerns only internal order, resolve it within your 6. If it affects a customer promise, bring it to me.
  13. Alex
    And if this creates more rework? I am happy to own the decision, but I will need your help getting another team involved.
  14. Sofia
    I retain management 7 for the trial. You are responsible for coordinating it, collecting the results, and raising issues promptly. I will help remove obstacles.
  15. Alex
    Could we put that on one page? Our old RACI chart still lists you as approving every change. People will follow that unless we replace it.
  16. Sofia
    Yes. Use plain role descriptions first. A matrix is useful only if the team understands the 8, not just the letters.
  17. Alex
    I will record the boundary as: internal sequence, Alex decides; customer promises or spending, Sofia approves. Support provides input before the sequence is chosen.
  18. Sofia
    That captures it. Add an 9: contact me before a proposed change would alter either a customer promise or the spending limit.
  19. Alex
    I will circulate that today and ask each named person to confirm their role. Then we can start the trial on Monday.
  20. Sofia
    And we will hold the 10 at the end of week two. Bring the rework count and the time taken; we will decide whether to keep, adjust, or stop the sequence.

Answers and explanations
  1. decision rights Turn 2. The missing information is who may decide which matters.
  2. delegated Turn 4. Sofia grants Alex authority for a defined period and scope.
  3. remit Turn 6. A remit is an assigned area of authority or work.
  4. exception Turn 8. A purchase falls outside the normal permission granted here.
  5. consulted Turn 10. Support provides input; Alex remains the decision owner for the internal sequence.
  6. discretion Turn 12. Discretion is the freedom to choose within the agreed limits.
  7. accountability Turn 14. Sofia keeps responsibility for oversight and support while Alex coordinates the work.
  8. responsibilities Turn 16. Knowing the actual duties matters more than memorizing the letters in a role map.
  9. escalation threshold Turn 18. The threshold identifies when Alex must take a proposed decision back to Sofia.
  10. review Turn 20. The two-week checkpoint evaluates the trial; it does not automatically approve continuation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: The team wants more freedom on onboarding, but "take ownership" has not been clear enough. Yesterday we waited for your reply before moving a routine internal check.
  2. Sofia: That is a fair criticism. I have asked for initiative without defining the decision rights. Let us separate what you can decide from what needs my approval.
  3. Alex: Our recommendation is to move the internal document check earlier. It would not change what we promise customers or require additional spending.
  4. Sofia: You have my permission to decide the internal task order. That is now delegated to you for the two-week trial; you do not need to ask about each change.
  5. Alex: Does that include changing a customer's promised completion date if an internal delay makes the original date difficult?
  6. Sofia: No. Customer promises are outside that remit. Bring those changes to me with the reason and the options before making a new commitment.
  7. Alex: Understood. And if the team wants a paid document-checking tool, we should stop at the spending boundary rather than order it and tell you afterward.
  8. Sofia: Exactly. Additional spending is an exception that needs approval. The team can research the tool and recommend it, but not purchase it under this delegation.
  9. Alex: I would like input from customer support before choosing the sequence. Do they need to agree, or is their role to identify problems we might miss?
  10. Sofia: For the internal sequence, they are consulted. You make the decision after using their input. Do not describe their silence as agreement.
  11. Alex: Then I will send them two options and ask for risks by Thursday noon. If they need more time, they can say so rather than approve by default.
  12. Sofia: Good. If a disagreement concerns only internal order, resolve it within your discretion. If it affects a customer promise, bring it to me.
  13. Alex: And if this creates more rework? I am happy to own the decision, but I will need your help getting another team involved.
  14. Sofia: I retain management accountability for the trial. You are responsible for coordinating it, collecting the results, and raising issues promptly. I will help remove obstacles.
  15. Alex: Could we put that on one page? Our old RACI chart still lists you as approving every change. People will follow that unless we replace it.
  16. Sofia: Yes. Use plain role descriptions first. A matrix is useful only if the team understands the responsibilities, not just the letters.
  17. Alex: I will record the boundary as: internal sequence, Alex decides; customer promises or spending, Sofia approves. Support provides input before the sequence is chosen.
  18. Sofia: That captures it. Add an escalation threshold: contact me before a proposed change would alter either a customer promise or the spending limit.
  19. Alex: I will circulate that today and ask each named person to confirm their role. Then we can start the trial on Monday.
  20. Sofia: And we will hold the review at the end of week two. Bring the rework count and the time taken; we will decide whether to keep, adjust, or stop the sequence.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Direction without answering every question

The manager in the lesson invites the new hire to discuss participation before the next team meeting.

  1. Branch manager: I've been answering every question, and you haven't offered ideas recently. I'd like your perspective.
  2. Operations associate: I'm unsure whether you want suggestions or only questions about decisions already made.
  3. Branch manager: That shows a role clarity problem. I haven't distinguished discussion from direction.
  4. Operations associate: Could you tell us which items are open before asking for contributions?
  5. Branch manager: Yes. I can identify open questions and explain whose decision rights apply.
  6. Operations associate: Would I be expected to defend an idea immediately in front of everyone?
  7. Branch manager: We can allow questions first. I don't want to treat an early suggestion as a commitment.
  8. Operations associate: Then I can contribute without pretending I have a fully developed proposal.
  9. Branch manager: I'll change the facilitation and invite other views before offering my own answer.
  10. Operations associate: Let's try that next meeting and check whether the invitation is clear.
A recommendation is not purchasing approval

A separate fictional branch: a facilities group may recommend furniture, while the branch director holds purchase approval.

  1. Facilities coordinator: The group preferred the adjustable desks. Can I tell the supplier to proceed?
  2. Branch director: Not yet. Your group has recommendation responsibility; I hold purchase approval in this project.
  3. Facilities coordinator: I thought unanimous support gave us decision rights over the order as well.
  4. Branch director: I should have made that distinction earlier. What concerns came up during the discussion?
  5. Facilities coordinator: We haven't heard from the reception team, which will also use the desks.
  6. Branch director: Then invite their perspective before treating the recommendation as complete.
  7. Facilities coordinator: Should I reopen every preference, or focus on their actual work requirements?
  8. Branch director: Focus on requirements and any unresolved concerns. We don't need another popularity vote.
  9. Facilities coordinator: I'll gather that input and bring a recommendation with the open questions identified.
  10. Branch director: Good. The supplier should receive no purchase instruction until I review it.
Who owns the shift decision?

A separate fictional branch: two supervisors each believe the other approves a proposed shift swap; no authority has been confirmed.

  1. Shift supervisor: The team discussed the swap, but I thought your office would approve it.
  2. Operations supervisor: I expected you to decide. We clearly need role clarity before giving an answer.
  3. Shift supervisor: Could we ask the operations manager to confirm the decision rights for this case?
  4. Operations supervisor: Yes. Meanwhile, have both affected shifts had a chance to explain their concerns?
  5. Shift supervisor: The morning team spoke, but the evening representative hasn't joined the discussion yet.
  6. Operations supervisor: Let's invite that perspective without implying that consultation itself authorizes the swap.
  7. Shift supervisor: What should I tell the staff members waiting for a response today?
  8. Operations supervisor: Say the request is under discussion and approval responsibility is still being clarified.
  9. Shift supervisor: I'll arrange the missing conversation and ask the manager who can decide.
  10. Operations supervisor: I'll keep the current arrangement unchanged until an authorized decision is communicated.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Delegating without disappearing
  1. Read the dialogue with one reader as Sofia and one as Alex. After turns 4, 6, and 10, repeat the stated permission or boundary exactly.
  2. Switch roles and read turns 15-20 as a decision-record check. Do not add authority that the record does not grant.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A conference booking

Sofia permits Alex to choose a room within an approved $2,000 budget. Changes to the event date require Sofia's approval. No extra spending is authorized.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Sofia: "You ___ choose the room within the approved budget."

Answer and explanation

C. may

"May" grants the specific permission described.

2. Alex: "If every suitable room costs more, I will ___ before booking."

Answer and explanation

D. seek your approval

A higher cost is outside the delegation, so approval must come before booking.

3. Sofia: "Changing the event date remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. my decision

Sofia expressly reserves the date decision.

4. Alex: "I will record the budget and the ___ so the team knows when to contact you."

Answer and explanation

D. approval boundary

The boundary explains which decisions need Sofia's approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 03: Make authority and participation clear
Original fictional case: Sofia has been approving every small change to an onboarding process. Alex wants the team to act faster but does not know where its authority ends. For this fictional project, Sofia can delegate changes to internal task order. Customer promises and additional spending still require her approval. A process trial will run for two weeks.
Language workshop: Delegating without disappearing
Communication goal: Define decision rights, invite recommendations, and delegate within explicit limits.
Language guidance: Consultation asks for input; consensus requires shared agreement. Delegation grants specified authority, not unlimited discretion. State the remit and escalation threshold before asking someone to take ownership.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Bring me your preferred option and the reason for it. / You may change the internal sequence without seeking approval each time. / Customer commitments remain my decision. / That discretion does not include additional spending. / If the change affects a customer promise, bring it back to me. / I need your input; I am not asking you to make the final decision. / I will ask each owner to confirm the responsibilities assigned to them. / We will review the trial after two weeks using the agreed criteria. / Within the agreed limits, the task order is yours to decide. / I am delegating the sequence, not stepping away from oversight. / Does this change fall within the authority you have delegated to me? / You may recommend the tool, but purchasing it still requires approval. / Let us name one person who is answerable for the trial result. / Raise the issue before making the customer commitment, not afterward. / The two-week review is a decision gate, not automatic permission to continue. / Please state which decisions you can make and which you will bring back to me.
Vocabulary: decision rights: Who may recommend, approve, or decide a particular matter. / delegation: Giving another person defined responsibility or authority. / discretion: Freedom to choose within stated limits. / guardrail: A limit intended to keep a decision within an acceptable range. / escalation threshold: The point at which an issue must go to another authority. / decision owner: The person accountable for making a particular decision. / consultation: Seeking input before an action or decision. / consensus: Broad agreement, which must not be inferred from silence alone. / accountability: Responsibility for explaining actions and following through. / remit: The area of work or authority assigned to a role. / exception: A case that falls outside the normal rule or arrangement. / RACI: A role map: Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, Informed. Organizations may apply it differently. / mandate: An assigned purpose and authority to carry out work. / autonomy: Freedom to organize or decide work within agreed limits. / approval boundary: The point beyond which a decision needs another person's permission. / retained authority: Decision power that is not passed to the person doing the work. / decision gate: A checkpoint at which a named owner decides whether work may proceed. / span of control: The number of people or teams directly supervised by one manager. / separation of duties: Dividing important tasks among roles to reduce conflicts or unchecked control. / oversight: Monitoring and review by someone responsible for the work. / accountable owner: The person answerable for a specified result or decision. / approval matrix: A table showing who can approve which decisions and limits. / revocable: Able to be withdrawn by the person or body granting it. / informed stakeholder: Someone who needs updates but does not necessarily decide.

Switch roles and read turns 15-20 as a decision-record check. Do not add authority that the record does not grant. Read the conference exchange using the selected options. Finish by repeating the budget and the reserved date decision.
Partner: Sofia: International customer-service manager / Alex: US-based team lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Negotiate realistic commitmentsFriday estimates or Monday verified figures+

Your goal

Offer bounded alternatives, use conditional commitments, and distinguish a forecast from a promise.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Friday estimates or Monday verified figures

Headquarters needs branch figures for a Friday planning meeting. Omar can send an estimate by 3 p.m. New York time on Friday or verified figures by noon New York time on Monday. The supplier reconciliation will not finish before Monday morning. Priya can choose the reporting option but cannot make reconciliation finish early. Estimates must be labeled as provisional.

Omar: International branch operations manager / Priya: Headquarters project sponsor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What can Omar supply on Friday?

Answer and explanation

B. A labeled estimate

The Friday deliverable is an estimate, not verified data.

2. What cannot Priya change simply by choosing an option?

Answer and explanation

C. The reconciliation completion constraint

Choosing an option does not make the missing check complete sooner.

3. Which wording preserves both the Friday deliverable and its time reference?

Answer and explanation

D. A provisional estimate Friday at 3 p.m. New York time

The agreed Friday output is provisional and the supplied clock reference is New York. The other choices change either its verification status or its time reference.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A deliverable is the output promised; a deadline is when it is due. A caveat states a limitation; a contingency states the fallback. An estimate can be useful without being verified, provided its status stays visible.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
deliverable
A specific output someone agrees to provide.define the deliverable
turnaround time
Time from a request or input to a completed response.agree a turnaround time
trade-off
A choice in which one benefit comes at the cost of another.make a trade-off
reconciliation
Checking records against one another and resolving differences.complete the reconciliation
verified
Checked against the required evidence or process.verified figures
estimate
An approximate figure based on available information.provide an estimate
caveat
A stated limitation or qualification.include a caveat
scope creep
Uncontrolled growth of the work beyond the agreed scope.prevent scope creep
contingency
A provision or alternative for a possible problem.plan a contingency
hard deadline
A deadline treated as fixed for the stated purpose.meet a hard deadline
best effort
An undertaking to try, not necessarily to deliver a result.make a best-effort attempt
overcommit
Promise more than the available capacity can support.avoid overcommitting
critical path
The sequence of dependent work that determines the earliest completion date.identify the critical path
buffer
Extra time or capacity kept available for variation or delay.build in a time buffer
slippage
Movement of a task or delivery beyond its planned date.report schedule slippage
baseline
An agreed starting plan used to compare later performance or changes.establish a schedule baseline
rebaseline
Formally replace an agreed plan with a revised reference plan.rebaseline the delivery schedule
dependency risk
The possibility that required work elsewhere will delay or block delivery.flag a dependency risk
minimum viable scope
The smallest set of work that can still meet the stated purpose.agree the minimum viable scope
rolling forecast
A forecast updated regularly as new information becomes available.maintain a rolling forecast
range estimate
An estimate expressed as a lower and upper value rather than a single figure.provide a range estimate
confidence level
Here, the stated strength of confidence in an estimate, with its basis explained.state the confidence level
resource allocation
The assignment of people, time, or other capacity to work.confirm resource allocation
acceptance criteria
The conditions an output must meet to be accepted as complete.agree acceptance criteria

A baseline records the agreed plan. Rebaselining changes that reference explicitly; it should not hide slippage. Here, "confidence level" is ordinary workplace language, not a calculated statistical confidence interval.

C Notice the language

A clear limit can protect trust

A quick "yes" may reduce tension in the call while creating a commitment the branch cannot meet. A vague "we will try" can also be heard as a promise. Name the deliverable, deadline, and condition separately. Offer a choice that serves the other person's purpose. Do not present willingness as capacity, or describe a required accuracy check as resistance to headquarters.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the purpose
What decision does the Friday meeting need to make?
Offer alternatives
We can provide an estimate on Friday or verified figures on Monday.
Make a conditional offer
We can meet Friday if the deliverable is an explicitly provisional estimate.
State a firm limit
I cannot commit to verified figures before the reconciliation is complete.
Protect the caveat
Please keep the provisional label when the figures are forwarded.
Check the deadline
Does "Friday" mean 3 p.m. New York time or your local end of day?
Separate effort from outcome
I can commit to the update; I cannot guarantee a result we do not control.
Confirm the choice
Then our agreement is Friday estimates, followed by Monday verification.
Ask what is essential
Which parts of the report are essential for Friday's decision?
Identify the dependency
The supplier check is on the critical path; extra formatting time will not shorten it.
Protect capacity
To include the other branches, we would need to move the date or reassign people.
Make uncertainty visible
I can give a range and explain its basis, but a single figure would imply false precision.
Agree what complete means
For this version, does "complete" mean ready for discussion or verified for release?
Report slippage early
The check is at risk of slipping; I will update you before the agreed deadline.
Preserve the original plan
Let us record the revised deadline alongside the original baseline.
Confirm a fallback
If verification is delayed, the fallback is a status update, not an unchecked final report.
6 language and precision notes
If + present tense
Use "If the scope stays the same, we can send the estimate on Friday." Do not use "if the scope will stay" for this ordinary future condition.
Provided that
"Provided that" makes a requirement explicit. It does not mean the requirement has already been met.
Cannot versus will not
"Cannot verify before Monday" describes a constraint. "Will not verify" can imply a refusal; select the meaning you intend.
By and until
"Send the report by Friday" sets the latest delivery time. "Keep the estimate provisional until Monday" describes a status continuing up to a point.
Subject to + noun
Use "subject to verification" or "subject to the supplier completing the check," not "subject to verify."
Meet versus deliver
You meet a deadline and deliver a report. "We can meet Friday" needs a clear deadline context; "we can deliver Friday's estimate" names the output.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Choose the commitment supported by the facts.

Answer and explanation

C. We can send a labeled estimate Friday at 3 p.m. New York time.

Only the labeled Friday estimate matches the stated capacity.

2. Complete the ordinary future condition: "If you ___ the estimate option, we can meet Friday."

Answer and explanation

A. choose

Use present simple in this conditional clause: "If you choose."

3. Which caveat preserves the status of the Friday figures?

Answer and explanation

B. Provisional; reconciliation pending

It states both the limitation and the outstanding check.

4. Which reply clarifies purpose before negotiating the deliverable?

Answer and explanation

D. Is Friday's decision about planning capacity or final reporting?

The needed decision helps determine whether an estimate can serve the meeting.

E Conversations

Friday estimates or Monday verified figures

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: International branch operations manager / Priya: Headquarters project sponsor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • caveat
  • trade-off
  • guarantee
  • scope creep
  • deliverable
  • provisional
  • provided that
  • commit
  • reconciliation
  • contingency
  1. Priya
    The planning group needs your branch figures on Friday. Can you send the final report before the meeting? We cannot move the meeting to Monday.
  2. Omar
    I can help with Friday, but I need to be clear about the 1. We can send an estimate then; Monday is our target for verified figures.
  3. Priya
    The deck needs one number. What is stopping you from checking it before Friday?
  4. Omar
    The supplier 2 will not finish before Monday morning. That is a missing check, not a choice to delay the report.
  5. Priya
    The group will use the number to compare staffing options. It is not making a final financial filing. Would an estimate be adequate for that discussion?
  6. Omar
    Yes, with a clear 3. I can provide the estimate, its basis, and the items not yet reconciled. I cannot describe it as verified.
  7. Priya
    Could you remove that warning from the main slide and put it in the notes? The slide is already crowded.
  8. Omar
    I need the 4 label to stay beside the figure. Otherwise readers may treat the estimate as final when the slide is forwarded without its notes.
  9. Priya
    Understood. Keep a short label on the slide and the detail in the notes. What is the earliest time you can send it?
  10. Omar
    Friday at 3 p.m. New York time, 5 the scope remains this branch and the current reporting period. Adding other branches would change the schedule.
  11. Priya
    That time works. I was about to add two other branches, but we can leave them out of this version. I will state that in the request.
  12. Omar
    Thank you. That avoids 6 after we have agreed the deadline. Please send the confirmed scope so I can brief the team accurately.
  13. Priya
    For Monday, can I tell the group that the verified number will definitely match Friday's estimate? A change could prompt more questions.
  14. Omar
    No. Verification may change it. I can 7 to explaining any difference; I cannot guarantee that the number itself will remain unchanged.
  15. Priya
    Then we need to explain that before the group makes a decision. I will frame Friday's figure as a planning input, not a certified result.
  16. Omar
    That is the right 8: speed for the planning meeting, followed by a checked result. We should not claim to have both on Friday.
  17. Priya
    What will you do if the supplier check is unexpectedly still incomplete on Monday morning? I would rather hear about a delay early.
  18. Omar
    Our 9 will be an explicit status update before noon, identifying what remains unresolved and when we can reassess. I will not relabel unchecked data as verified.
  19. Priya
    Agreed. I will record the Friday estimate and Monday verified-report target, with that fallback if the supplier check is delayed.
  20. Omar
    Please also record that the Friday estimate is not a 10 of Monday's outcome. With that wording, I can confirm the commitment and brief the team.

Answers and explanations
  1. deliverable Turn 2. The distinction is between two outputs: an estimate and a verified report.
  2. reconciliation Turn 4. Comparing supplier records and resolving differences is reconciliation.
  3. caveat Turn 6. The estimate needs a stated limitation, not an assurance that it is final.
  4. provisional Turn 8. The visible label must show that the figure is not yet final.
  5. provided that Turn 10. The delivery promise depends on unchanged scope; this phrase states that condition.
  6. scope creep Turn 12. Adding two branches would expand the agreed work after the deadline was set.
  7. commit Turn 14. "Commit to" names the undertaking Omar can make: explaining a difference.
  8. trade-off Turn 16. The team exchanges early availability for provisional rather than verified information.
  9. contingency Turn 18. The fallback is a status update if the expected supplier check is delayed.
  10. guarantee Turn 20. The Friday number must not be understood as an assurance of the eventual result.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: The planning group needs your branch figures on Friday. Can you send the final report before the meeting? We cannot move the meeting to Monday.
  2. Omar: I can help with Friday, but I need to be clear about the deliverable. We can send an estimate then; Monday is our target for verified figures.
  3. Priya: The deck needs one number. What is stopping you from checking it before Friday?
  4. Omar: The supplier reconciliation will not finish before Monday morning. That is a missing check, not a choice to delay the report.
  5. Priya: The group will use the number to compare staffing options. It is not making a final financial filing. Would an estimate be adequate for that discussion?
  6. Omar: Yes, with a clear caveat. I can provide the estimate, its basis, and the items not yet reconciled. I cannot describe it as verified.
  7. Priya: Could you remove that warning from the main slide and put it in the notes? The slide is already crowded.
  8. Omar: I need the provisional label to stay beside the figure. Otherwise readers may treat the estimate as final when the slide is forwarded without its notes.
  9. Priya: Understood. Keep a short label on the slide and the detail in the notes. What is the earliest time you can send it?
  10. Omar: Friday at 3 p.m. New York time, provided that the scope remains this branch and the current reporting period. Adding other branches would change the schedule.
  11. Priya: That time works. I was about to add two other branches, but we can leave them out of this version. I will state that in the request.
  12. Omar: Thank you. That avoids scope creep after we have agreed the deadline. Please send the confirmed scope so I can brief the team accurately.
  13. Priya: For Monday, can I tell the group that the verified number will definitely match Friday's estimate? A change could prompt more questions.
  14. Omar: No. Verification may change it. I can commit to explaining any difference; I cannot guarantee that the number itself will remain unchanged.
  15. Priya: Then we need to explain that before the group makes a decision. I will frame Friday's figure as a planning input, not a certified result.
  16. Omar: That is the right trade-off: speed for the planning meeting, followed by a checked result. We should not claim to have both on Friday.
  17. Priya: What will you do if the supplier check is unexpectedly still incomplete on Monday morning? I would rather hear about a delay early.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Friday estimate or Monday verification

Headquarters and the branch negotiate the two reporting options supplied in the lesson.

  1. Headquarters analyst: We requested the report for Friday. Can your branch make that commitment?
  2. Branch reporting lead: We can provide a clearly labeled estimate Friday or verified figures Monday.
  3. Headquarters analyst: The review meeting is waiting on information. Could Friday's estimate be presented as final?
  4. Branch reporting lead: No. That would hide the tradeoff between the deadline and the verification status.
  5. Headquarters analyst: What would we be able to say accurately about the Friday version?
  6. Branch reporting lead: That it is an estimate and that verified figures are available Monday.
  7. Headquarters analyst: Could we use the estimate for discussion, then replace it with the verified report?
  8. Branch reporting lead: Yes, provided the provisional status stays visible wherever the estimate is shared.
  9. Headquarters analyst: I'll take that condition to the meeting owner before confirming their preferred option.
  10. Branch reporting lead: Then let me know the choice. I won't describe Friday's figures as verified.
A smaller orientation session

A separate fictional branch has one trainer available for a two-hour session; the requested full orientation takes four hours.

  1. Hiring manager: Can you fit the complete orientation into the two hours the trainer has?
  2. Training lead: The current program takes four hours. Compressing it would change what participants receive.
  3. Hiring manager: What practical option can you offer without promising another trainer we don't have?
  4. Training lead: We could propose a two-hour introduction and arrange the remaining content separately.
  5. Hiring manager: Would that mean new hires had completed the full orientation on day one?
  6. Training lead: No. We would need to identify the unfinished sections and agree their timing.
  7. Hiring manager: I can accept a staged approach, but I need the remaining session date reviewed.
  8. Training lead: I'll check availability before making that commitment. The second date isn't confirmed.
  9. Hiring manager: Please describe the scope tradeoff clearly when you bring the proposal back.
  10. Training lead: I will. For now, we have a possible introduction, not a shortened full program.
The visit agenda exceeds the available hour

A separate fictional branch visit has a confirmed one-hour discussion slot and six requested topics, each allocated fifteen minutes.

  1. Branch host: Six fifteen-minute topics need ninety minutes, but the visit includes only one hour.
  2. Regional director: Can we keep all six and simply speak faster to meet the deadline?
  3. Branch host: That risks losing the discussion. We could select four topics or seek a longer slot.
  4. Regional director: The visit schedule is fixed. Could the remaining two become a follow-up conversation?
  5. Branch host: Yes, if their owners agree. I can't commit their calendars on their behalf.
  6. Regional director: Then let me prioritize four topics, and you can ask about the follow-up.
  7. Branch host: Should we mark the other two as deferred rather than silently remove them?
  8. Regional director: Definitely. I want a clear tradeoff, not the impression that every issue was covered.
  9. Branch host: I'll circulate the revised scope and keep the follow-up timing unconfirmed.
  10. Regional director: Good. Our commitment is four topics in one hour, with two still to schedule.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Friday estimates or Monday verified figures
  1. Read the full dialogue, pausing after each offer so the listener can repeat its deadline and condition.
  2. Switch roles and reread turns 7-14. Keep the distinction between a provisional figure and a guaranteed result.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A presentation with an unfinished review

A client presentation is requested Tuesday. A ten-slide internal draft can be ready then. Compliance review is scheduled to finish Wednesday, but clearance has not been granted. Only an explicitly cleared version may be sent externally.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Priya: "Can you deliver ten slides Tuesday?" Omar: "Yes, as an ___ draft."

Answer and explanation

C. internal

An internal draft is available; external release must wait for review.

2. Priya: "Can the client receive it then?" Omar: "Not ___ compliance grants clearance."

Answer and explanation

C. until

"Not until" makes actual clearance a condition of release; a scheduled review date does not establish approval.

3. Priya: "Then I will call Tuesday's file ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review pending

The label accurately preserves the outstanding review.

4. Omar: "If compliance ___ the version Wednesday, we can prepare it for release."

Answer and explanation

C. clears

The future condition takes present simple: "clears." The action depends on actual clearance, not merely completion of a review.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Negotiate realistic commitments
Original fictional case: Headquarters needs branch figures for a Friday planning meeting. Omar can send an estimate by 3 p.m. New York time on Friday or verified figures by noon New York time on Monday. The supplier reconciliation will not finish before Monday morning. Priya can choose the reporting option but cannot make reconciliation finish early. Estimates must be labeled as provisional.
Language workshop: Friday estimates or Monday verified figures
Communication goal: Offer bounded alternatives, use conditional commitments, and distinguish a forecast from a promise.
Language guidance: A deliverable is the output promised; a deadline is when it is due. A caveat states a limitation; a contingency states the fallback. An estimate can be useful without being verified, provided its status stays visible.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What decision does the Friday meeting need to make? / We can provide an estimate on Friday or verified figures on Monday. / We can meet Friday if the deliverable is an explicitly provisional estimate. / I cannot commit to verified figures before the reconciliation is complete. / Please keep the provisional label when the figures are forwarded. / Does "Friday" mean 3 p.m. New York time or your local end of day? / I can commit to the update; I cannot guarantee a result we do not control. / Then our agreement is Friday estimates, followed by Monday verification. / Which parts of the report are essential for Friday's decision? / The supplier check is on the critical path; extra formatting time will not shorten it. / To include the other branches, we would need to move the date or reassign people. / I can give a range and explain its basis, but a single figure would imply false precision. / For this version, does "complete" mean ready for discussion or verified for release? / The check is at risk of slipping; I will update you before the agreed deadline. / Let us record the revised deadline alongside the original baseline. / If verification is delayed, the fallback is a status update, not an unchecked final report.
Vocabulary: deliverable: A specific output someone agrees to provide. / turnaround time: Time from a request or input to a completed response. / trade-off: A choice in which one benefit comes at the cost of another. / reconciliation: Checking records against one another and resolving differences. / verified: Checked against the required evidence or process. / estimate: An approximate figure based on available information. / caveat: A stated limitation or qualification. / scope creep: Uncontrolled growth of the work beyond the agreed scope. / contingency: A provision or alternative for a possible problem. / hard deadline: A deadline treated as fixed for the stated purpose. / best effort: An undertaking to try, not necessarily to deliver a result. / overcommit: Promise more than the available capacity can support. / critical path: The sequence of dependent work that determines the earliest completion date. / buffer: Extra time or capacity kept available for variation or delay. / slippage: Movement of a task or delivery beyond its planned date. / baseline: An agreed starting plan used to compare later performance or changes. / rebaseline: Formally replace an agreed plan with a revised reference plan. / dependency risk: The possibility that required work elsewhere will delay or block delivery. / minimum viable scope: The smallest set of work that can still meet the stated purpose. / rolling forecast: A forecast updated regularly as new information becomes available. / range estimate: An estimate expressed as a lower and upper value rather than a single figure. / confidence level: Here, the stated strength of confidence in an estimate, with its basis explained. / resource allocation: The assignment of people, time, or other capacity to work. / acceptance criteria: The conditions an output must meet to be accepted as complete.
Speaking task: Read the full dialogue, pausing after each offer so the listener can repeat its deadline and condition. Switch roles and reread turns 7-14. Keep the distinction between a provisional figure and a guaranteed result. Read the presentation exchange with the selected words. Finish by repeating which file may be sent externally.
Partner: Omar: International branch operations manager / Priya: Headquarters project sponsor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client presentation is requested Tuesday. A ten-slide internal draft can be ready then. Compliance review is scheduled to finish Wednesday, but clearance has not been granted. Only an explicitly cleared version may be sent externally.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: What will you do if the supplier check is unexpectedly still incomplete on Monday morning? I would rather hear about a delay early.
Omar: Our contingency will be an explicit status update before noon, identifying what remains unresolved and when we can reassess. I will not relabel unchecked data as verified.
Priya: Agreed. I will record the Friday estimate and Monday verified-report target, with that fallback if the supplier check is delayed.
Omar: Please also record that the Friday estimate is not a guarantee of Monday's outcome. With that wording, I can confirm the commitment and brief the team.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Cross-Cultural Leadership English
Professional audience: managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators
Scope: Ask about individual preferences and team norms. Nationality, accent, silence, or directness does not tell you what a person believes or how they will behave.
Lesson 04: Negotiate realistic commitments
Original fictional case: Headquarters needs branch figures for a Friday planning meeting. Omar can send an estimate by 3 p.m. New York time on Friday or verified figures by noon New York time on Monday. The supplier reconciliation will not finish before Monday morning. Priya can choose the reporting option but cannot make reconciliation finish early. Estimates must be labeled as provisional.
Language workshop: Friday estimates or Monday verified figures
Communication goal: Offer bounded alternatives, use conditional commitments, and distinguish a forecast from a promise.
Language guidance: A deliverable is the output promised; a deadline is when it is due. A caveat states a limitation; a contingency states the fallback. An estimate can be useful without being verified, provided its status stays visible.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What decision does the Friday meeting need to make? / We can provide an estimate on Friday or verified figures on Monday. / We can meet Friday if the deliverable is an explicitly provisional estimate. / I cannot commit to verified figures before the reconciliation is complete. / Please keep the provisional label when the figures are forwarded. / Does "Friday" mean 3 p.m. New York time or your local end of day? / I can commit to the update; I cannot guarantee a result we do not control. / Then our agreement is Friday estimates, followed by Monday verification. / Which parts of the report are essential for Friday's decision? / The supplier check is on the critical path; extra formatting time will not shorten it. / To include the other branches, we would need to move the date or reassign people. / I can give a range and explain its basis, but a single figure would imply false precision. / For this version, does "complete" mean ready for discussion or verified for release? / The check is at risk of slipping; I will update you before the agreed deadline. / Let us record the revised deadline alongside the original baseline. / If verification is delayed, the fallback is a status update, not an unchecked final report.
Vocabulary: deliverable: A specific output someone agrees to provide. / turnaround time: Time from a request or input to a completed response. / trade-off: A choice in which one benefit comes at the cost of another. / reconciliation: Checking records against one another and resolving differences. / verified: Checked against the required evidence or process. / estimate: An approximate figure based on available information. / caveat: A stated limitation or qualification. / scope creep: Uncontrolled growth of the work beyond the agreed scope. / contingency: A provision or alternative for a possible problem. / hard deadline: A deadline treated as fixed for the stated purpose. / best effort: An undertaking to try, not necessarily to deliver a result. / overcommit: Promise more than the available capacity can support. / critical path: The sequence of dependent work that determines the earliest completion date. / buffer: Extra time or capacity kept available for variation or delay. / slippage: Movement of a task or delivery beyond its planned date. / baseline: An agreed starting plan used to compare later performance or changes. / rebaseline: Formally replace an agreed plan with a revised reference plan. / dependency risk: The possibility that required work elsewhere will delay or block delivery. / minimum viable scope: The smallest set of work that can still meet the stated purpose. / rolling forecast: A forecast updated regularly as new information becomes available. / range estimate: An estimate expressed as a lower and upper value rather than a single figure. / confidence level: Here, the stated strength of confidence in an estimate, with its basis explained. / resource allocation: The assignment of people, time, or other capacity to work. / acceptance criteria: The conditions an output must meet to be accepted as complete.
Speaking task: Read the full dialogue, pausing after each offer so the listener can repeat its deadline and condition. Switch roles and reread turns 7-14. Keep the distinction between a provisional figure and a guaranteed result. Read the presentation exchange with the selected words. Finish by repeating which file may be sent externally.
Partner: Omar: International branch operations manager / Priya: Headquarters project sponsor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client presentation is requested Tuesday. A ten-slide internal draft can be ready then. Compliance review is scheduled to finish Wednesday, but clearance has not been granted. Only an explicitly cleared version may be sent externally.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Priya: What will you do if the supplier check is unexpectedly still incomplete on Monday morning? I would rather hear about a delay early.
Omar: Our contingency will be an explicit status update before noon, identifying what remains unresolved and when we can reassess. I will not relabel unchecked data as verified.
Priya: Agreed. I will record the Friday estimate and Monday verified-report target, with that fallback if the supplier check is delayed.
Omar: Please also record that the Friday estimate is not a guarantee of Monday's outcome. With that wording, I can confirm the commitment and brief the team.
END REFERENCE
05Include every voice in a meetingThe remote question nobody heard+

Your goal

Manage turn-taking, interrupt constructively, paraphrase accurately, and close a hybrid meeting.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The remote question nobody heard

Three participants have been speaking over one another. Arun posted a concern in the meeting chat, but nobody responded. The team has fifteen minutes left. The new queue can go live only after routing is checked; Arun has not completed that check. Leila owns the go-live decision, and Ben has been preparing the customer-service schedule.

Leila: International manager and meeting chair / Ben: US-based service lead / Arun: Remote systems lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Whose input has been missed?

Answer and explanation

B. Arun's routing concern

Arun posted a concern in chat without receiving a response.

2. What blocks a final go-live decision?

Answer and explanation

C. An unfinished routing check

The check is a stated prerequisite and is not complete.

3. Who owns the go-live decision?

Answer and explanation

A. Leila

The meeting chair also holds the decision authority in this case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Giving someone the floor transfers the speaking turn, not decision authority. An action item assigns work; a decision log records what has been decided or remains pending. Neither silence nor attendance establishes approval.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
turn-taking
The way speakers enter, hold, and give up a speaking turn.manage turn-taking
the floor
The opportunity to speak to the group.give someone the floor
crosstalk
Overlapping speech that makes contributions hard to follow.reduce crosstalk
timebox
A fixed amount of time allocated to a discussion or task.timebox the discussion
round-robin
A sequence in which each participant is invited to contribute.use a round-robin
paraphrase
Restate someone's meaning in different words.paraphrase a concern
parking lot
An informal list of issues deferred for later attention.put an item in the parking lot
dissent
A stated view that differs from the proposal or majority view.record dissent
quorum
The minimum attendance required under a group's rules for valid business.confirm a quorum
action item
A specific task recorded for follow-up.assign an action item
decision log
A record of decisions, owners, and relevant reasons or conditions.update the decision log
asynchronous input
A contribution made outside a simultaneous meeting.collect asynchronous input
facilitation
Helping a group follow a workable process and make progress.provide neutral facilitation
speaking queue
The order in which waiting participants will be invited to speak.manage the speaking queue
airtime
The amount of meeting time a person uses to speak.share airtime fairly
agenda item
A specific topic scheduled for discussion.close an agenda item
unresolved question
A question that has not yet received an adequate answer.return to an unresolved question
synthesis
A summary that connects important points from several contributions.offer a concise synthesis
cross-check
Compare information with another source or person to check accuracy.cross-check the meeting summary
recap
A short restatement of the main points just covered.give a brief recap
side conversation
A separate exchange that can distract from the main discussion.pause a side conversation
deferred item
An issue deliberately moved to a later time.assign an owner to a deferred item
meeting owner
The person responsible for arranging and following through on a meeting.confirm the meeting owner
pre-read
Material circulated for participants to read before a meeting.circulate the pre-read

A recap restates the main points; a synthesis connects contributions. A deferred item needs a return point and an owner. Giving access to a discussion does not require treating every contribution as approval.

C Notice the language

Protect access, not just airtime

Someone may be quiet because of audio delay, language-processing time, a missed invitation, or uncertainty about the decision. Do not diagnose the reason from silence. Give the remote participant a named turn, stop overlapping speech, and summarize the contribution before moving on. First-name address or an informal manner does not establish that hierarchy has disappeared. The chair still needs to state who decides and whose input is missing.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Stop overlap
One voice at a time, please; the remote line cannot distinguish us.
Protect a turn
Arun has not finished. I will come to you next, Ben.
Enter the discussion
May I come in on the routing point before we move on?
Invite a specific view
Arun, what remains unverified in the routing check?
Check a paraphrase
Have I represented your concern accurately?
Limit a digression
Let us park the training format and finish the go-live conditions.
Record dissent
I will record the concern without describing it as agreement.
Close with ownership
The next action belongs to Arun; the go-live decision remains with me.
Manage the queue
I have Ben, then Arun, then Leila in the speaking queue.
Pause a side exchange
Please pause the side conversation so everyone can hear the answer.
Include a missed voice
We have not heard from the remote team yet; what would you add on routing?
Connect two contributions
Ben has confirmed the staffing; Arun has identified a separate routing condition.
Check access to information
Can everyone see the same version of the pre-read?
Keep a deferred item alive
We will defer the slide layout, with Ben bringing it back on Thursday.
Preserve an objection
The summary will show that the scheduling proposal still has an unresolved objection.
Close an item accurately
We have assigned the checks, but we have not approved go-live.
6 language and precision notes
A useful interruption
State the reason briefly: "Before we move on, Arun's question is still open." This protects an unresolved issue rather than merely taking airtime.
Paraphrase versus infer
"You said the check is incomplete" reports a contribution. "You do not support the project" adds a position the speaker may not hold.
An invitation that can be answered
"Any thoughts?" is broad. "What remains unverified?" gives the invited speaker a precise task.
Invite without diagnosing
"We have not heard your view on routing" invites a contribution. "You are quiet because you disagree" assumes both a reason and a position.
Protect the next turn
"Please finish, Arun; Ben is next" preserves the current speaker and gives the waiting speaker a clear place.
Close the right thing
"We have finished discussing the checks" closes a discussion. "We have approved go-live" records a decision. One does not automatically imply the other.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Choose the intervention that protects the interrupted speaker.

Answer and explanation

C. Arun has not finished; Ben will speak next.

It preserves Arun's turn and gives Ben a predictable next turn.

2. Which paraphrase does not add an unsupported conclusion?

Answer and explanation

B. Arun said the routing check is incomplete.

It restates the known information without inferring motive or approval.

3. Which action belongs in a decision log?

Answer and explanation

D. Go-live pending; Arun checks routing; Leila decides afterward.

It records status, work ownership, and decision authority accurately.

4. Which issue must not be parked before this go-live decision?

Answer and explanation

A. The unfinished routing check

Routing is the stated go-live prerequisite. The other improvements are not supplied as conditions for this decision.

The remote question nobody heard

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leila: International manager and meeting chair / Ben: US-based service lead / Arun: Remote systems lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Ben
    If we start Monday, the schedule is ready. I think we have covered the main points, so can we confirm the switch and send the roster?
  2. Leila
    Before we confirm anything, we need to stop the 1. Three people were speaking, and I could not hear the remote contribution.
  3. Arun
    Thank you. I put a question in the chat about routing, but the discussion moved to staffing. I am not saying the schedule is wrong; the technical check is still open.
  4. Ben
    I saw a message appear, but I did not read it fully. Arun, you have 2. What is the unresolved part?
  5. Arun
    We have checked new tickets, but not tickets transferred from the old queue. If those transfers are misrouted, the schedule will not prevent delays.
  6. Leila
    Let me 3 that: new tickets have passed the check; transferred tickets have not. So we cannot treat the complete routing check as finished. Is that accurate?
  7. Arun
    Yes. I need a test set of transferred tickets and someone from service to confirm that they reach the intended team.
  8. Ben
    I can supply that test set. But could we first discuss the training slides? There are several changes I would like to make to the layout.
  9. Leila
    Let us put the slide layout in the 4. We have fifteen minutes for the go-live conditions, and the routing check is a prerequisite.
  10. Arun
    That helps. I need ten representative transferred tickets, not another overview of the training. Ben, can your team provide those by 11 a.m. tomorrow?
  11. Ben
    Yes, by eleven tomorrow. And I should correct my opening: the roster is ready, but I am not the person who approves the switch.
  12. Leila
    Correct. The go-live 5 is mine. Preparing the schedule is an action, not an approval. I will make that distinction in the record.
  13. Arun
    May I add one more condition? I need a service colleague in the test, otherwise we will know the technical destination but not whether the operational routing is useful.
  14. Leila
    Yes. I will give you a short 6 to finish the conditions, then we will confirm the owner of each task. Ben, please hold your reply until Arun finishes.
  15. Arun
    That is the full list: representative transfers and a service colleague. If those are available tomorrow, I can report the test result that afternoon.
  16. Ben
    I will name the service colleague and supply the ten tickets. Please record both as my 7, with the 11 a.m. deadline.
  17. Leila
    Recorded. Arun reports the test result tomorrow afternoon; I decide afterward. Until then, the proposed switch remains pending, not approved.
  18. Arun
    Please also record my 8 on approving Monday today. I support testing the switch; I do not support releasing it before the check.
  19. Ben
    That distinction is clear. I will hold the announcement. If anyone misses the remaining discussion, I will collect their input in the shared note rather than infer agreement.
  20. Leila
    Good. The 9 will show the pending decision and named owners. We will accept 10 until tomorrow's check so absent colleagues can identify relevant risks.

Answers and explanations
  1. crosstalk Turn 2. Overlapping voices prevented the remote contribution from being heard.
  2. the floor Turn 4. Ben explicitly gives Arun the opportunity to speak to the group.
  3. paraphrase Turn 6. Leila restates the distinction and asks Arun to verify it.
  4. parking lot Turn 9. The slide layout is deferred; the prerequisite routing issue is not.
  5. decision Turn 12. Leila retains authority to approve the switch.
  6. timebox Turn 14. A short protected period gives Arun space to finish without letting the meeting drift.
  7. action items Turn 16. Ben takes responsibility for two specific follow-up tasks.
  8. dissent Turn 18. Arun opposes final approval today, not the entire project.
  9. decision log Turn 20. This record distinguishes a pending decision from assigned work.
  10. asynchronous input Turn 20. Colleagues can contribute in the shared note without joining the live meeting.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: If we start Monday, the schedule is ready. I think we have covered the main points, so can we confirm the switch and send the roster?
  2. Leila: Before we confirm anything, we need to stop the crosstalk. Three people were speaking, and I could not hear the remote contribution.
  3. Arun: Thank you. I put a question in the chat about routing, but the discussion moved to staffing. I am not saying the schedule is wrong; the technical check is still open.
  4. Ben: I saw a message appear, but I did not read it fully. Arun, you have the floor. What is the unresolved part?
  5. Arun: We have checked new tickets, but not tickets transferred from the old queue. If those transfers are misrouted, the schedule will not prevent delays.
  6. Leila: Let me paraphrase that: new tickets have passed the check; transferred tickets have not. So we cannot treat the complete routing check as finished. Is that accurate?
  7. Arun: Yes. I need a test set of transferred tickets and someone from service to confirm that they reach the intended team.
  8. Ben: I can supply that test set. But could we first discuss the training slides? There are several changes I would like to make to the layout.
  9. Leila: Let us put the slide layout in the parking lot. We have fifteen minutes for the go-live conditions, and the routing check is a prerequisite.
  10. Arun: That helps. I need ten representative transferred tickets, not another overview of the training. Ben, can your team provide those by 11 a.m. tomorrow?
  11. Ben: Yes, by eleven tomorrow. And I should correct my opening: the roster is ready, but I am not the person who approves the switch.
  12. Leila: Correct. The go-live decision is mine. Preparing the schedule is an action, not an approval. I will make that distinction in the record.
  13. Arun: May I add one more condition? I need a service colleague in the test, otherwise we will know the technical destination but not whether the operational routing is useful.
  14. Leila: Yes. I will give you a short timebox to finish the conditions, then we will confirm the owner of each task. Ben, please hold your reply until Arun finishes.
  15. Arun: That is the full list: representative transfers and a service colleague. If those are available tomorrow, I can report the test result that afternoon.
  16. Ben: I will name the service colleague and supply the ten tickets. Please record both as my action items, with the 11 a.m. deadline.
  17. Leila: Recorded. Arun reports the test result tomorrow afternoon; I decide afterward. Until then, the proposed switch remains pending, not approved.
  18. Arun: Please also record my dissent on approving Monday today. I support testing the switch; I do not support releasing it before the check.
  19. Ben: That distinction is clear. I will hold the announcement. If anyone misses the remaining discussion, I will collect their input in the shared note rather than infer agreement.
  20. Leila: Good. The decision log will show the pending decision and named owners. We will accept asynchronous input until tomorrow's check so absent colleagues can identify relevant risks.
