Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Recovering the remote question

The lesson's hybrid meeting has overlapping speakers, a missed remote question, and unclear approval responsibility.

  1. Meeting chair: Let's pause the overlapping discussion. Our remote colleague had a question we didn't hear.
  2. Remote project lead: Thank you. Before discussing implementation, I need to know who can approve this plan.
  3. Meeting chair: That hasn't been established. I won't record approval in the meeting minutes.
  4. Remote project lead: Could someone take an action item to confirm the decision owner?
  5. Meeting chair: I'll take that action. Is there another concern we missed while people were speaking?
  6. Remote project lead: I'd like each speaker to finish before the next person responds.
  7. Meeting chair: Agreed. I'll call on people in turn and check the remote participants before closing.
  8. Remote project lead: Does that mean today's discussion can still produce recommendations without a final decision?
  9. Meeting chair: Yes. We'll distinguish recommendations, unresolved questions, and the pending approval route.
  10. Remote project lead: Then please read that distinction back at the end so everyone can confirm it.
A chat comment omitted from the summary

A separate fictional meeting: a warehouse representative posted a delivery-access concern in chat, but the chair's summary omitted it.

  1. Warehouse representative: My delivery-access concern was in the chat, but it isn't in your summary.
  2. Meeting chair: Thank you for catching that. Could you explain it aloud before we close?
  3. Warehouse representative: I want the team to check delivery access before recommending the new reception layout.
  4. Meeting chair: So the concern is an unresolved access check, not a rejection of the entire layout?
  5. Warehouse representative: Exactly. Please don't record me as either fully supportive or opposed.
  6. Meeting chair: I'll add the concern to the meeting minutes and ask who can investigate.
  7. Warehouse representative: I can describe the delivery workflow, but facilities needs to assess the layout.
  8. Meeting chair: Then your action item is to explain the workflow, not certify the layout.
  9. Warehouse representative: That is accurate. Could we invite facilities before choosing an implementation date?
  10. Meeting chair: Yes. I'll request their input and leave the recommendation conditional on that discussion.
The timer and the unanswered question

A separate fictional planning call has three minutes remaining; a service coordinator has been waiting to ask about staffing.

  1. Service coordinator: We have three minutes left, and I still haven't asked my staffing question.
  2. Meeting facilitator: Let's protect a minute for it. We should not close by assuming silence means agreement.
  3. Service coordinator: Who will assess coverage before this plan is recommended to the branch director?
  4. Meeting facilitator: We haven't assigned that action item. Are you able to describe the coverage requirements?
  5. Service coordinator: Yes, but I can't confirm the staffing allocation without the scheduling lead.
  6. Meeting facilitator: Then I'll invite that lead to a follow-up rather than name you as approver.
  7. Service coordinator: Please make that distinction in the meeting minutes; it affects what others expect.
  8. Meeting facilitator: I'll record your input task and the unresolved allocation question separately.
  9. Service coordinator: Can you read those two points now so I can check them?
  10. Meeting facilitator: You describe coverage needs; the scheduling lead's input and the director's decision remain pending.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 05: Include every voice in a meeting
Original fictional case: Three participants have been speaking over one another. Arun posted a concern in the meeting chat, but nobody responded. The team has fifteen minutes left. The new queue can go live only after routing is checked; Arun has not completed that check. Leila owns the go-live decision, and Ben has been preparing the customer-service schedule.
Language workshop: The remote question nobody heard
Communication goal: Manage turn-taking, interrupt constructively, paraphrase accurately, and close a hybrid meeting.
Language guidance: Giving someone the floor transfers the speaking turn, not decision authority. An action item assigns work; a decision log records what has been decided or remains pending. Neither silence nor attendance establishes approval.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): One voice at a time, please; the remote line cannot distinguish us. / Arun has not finished. I will come to you next, Ben. / May I come in on the routing point before we move on? / Arun, what remains unverified in the routing check? / Have I represented your concern accurately? / Let us park the training format and finish the go-live conditions. / I will record the concern without describing it as agreement. / The next action belongs to Arun; the go-live decision remains with me. / I have Ben, then Arun, then Leila in the speaking queue. / Please pause the side conversation so everyone can hear the answer. / We have not heard from the remote team yet; what would you add on routing? / Ben has confirmed the staffing; Arun has identified a separate routing condition. / Can everyone see the same version of the pre-read? / We will defer the slide layout, with Ben bringing it back on Thursday. / The summary will show that the scheduling proposal still has an unresolved objection. / We have assigned the checks, but we have not approved go-live.
Vocabulary: turn-taking: The way speakers enter, hold, and give up a speaking turn. / the floor: The opportunity to speak to the group. / crosstalk: Overlapping speech that makes contributions hard to follow. / timebox: A fixed amount of time allocated to a discussion or task. / round-robin: A sequence in which each participant is invited to contribute. / paraphrase: Restate someone's meaning in different words. / parking lot: An informal list of issues deferred for later attention. / dissent: A stated view that differs from the proposal or majority view. / quorum: The minimum attendance required under a group's rules for valid business. / action item: A specific task recorded for follow-up. / decision log: A record of decisions, owners, and relevant reasons or conditions. / asynchronous input: A contribution made outside a simultaneous meeting. / facilitation: Helping a group follow a workable process and make progress. / speaking queue: The order in which waiting participants will be invited to speak. / airtime: The amount of meeting time a person uses to speak. / agenda item: A specific topic scheduled for discussion. / unresolved question: A question that has not yet received an adequate answer. / synthesis: A summary that connects important points from several contributions. / cross-check: Compare information with another source or person to check accuracy. / recap: A short restatement of the main points just covered. / side conversation: A separate exchange that can distract from the main discussion. / deferred item: An issue deliberately moved to a later time. / meeting owner: The person responsible for arranging and following through on a meeting. / pre-read: Material circulated for participants to read before a meeting.

On the second reading, pause before "Is that accurate?" and wait for the printed confirmation. Read the video-call exchange, then repeat the four completed lines without changing "pending" to "approved."

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Give specific, useful feedbackTwo late reports and one missing handoff+

Your goal

Describe behavior, explain operational impact, ask about causes, and agree a measurable next step.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Two late reports and one missing handoff

The weekly report is due at noon on Thursday. In each of the last two weeks it arrived on Friday morning. Planning had to defer a staffing update. Elena does not know why the reports were late. Diego depends on a source file from another team. This is a coaching discussion, not a fictional disciplinary procedure.

Elena: International department manager / Diego: US-based analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which statement describes an observation?

Answer and explanation

C. The last two reports arrived Friday morning.

Arrival dates are observable; the other statements infer motives or overgeneralize.

2. What operational effect is known?

Answer and explanation

A. Planning deferred its staffing update.

Only the deferred staffing update is supplied by the case.

3. What should Elena avoid assuming before asking?

Answer and explanation

B. The cause of the delay.

The timing and dependency are known; the reason for lateness is not.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An impact is an effect you can describe; an attribution is an explanation you assign. Check the explanation before treating it as a fact. A check-in reviews progress; follow-through means carrying out the agreed action.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
observable behavior
An action that can be described without guessing at motive.describe observable behavior
impact
The effect of an action on people or work.explain the impact
attribution
An explanation assigned to someone's behavior or an event.check an attribution
pattern
A repeated feature across more than one event.identify a pattern
bottleneck
A point that limits the flow or speed of work.remove a bottleneck
upstream input
Information or work required from an earlier stage or another team.obtain upstream input
lead time
The interval needed before an output or action can be ready.allow sufficient lead time
corrective action
An agreed step to address a problem or prevent its recurrence.agree corrective action
check-in
A brief conversation to review status or provide support.schedule a check-in
follow-through
Carrying out what was agreed, not just agreeing to it.demonstrate follow-through
performance expectation
A clear description of the work standard required.clarify a performance expectation
SBI
Situation-Behavior-Impact: CCL's sequence for specific feedback.use an SBI structure [2]
coaching
Support that helps someone improve specific work or skills.hold a coaching conversation
performance gap
The difference between an agreed standard and observed performance.identify a performance gap
root cause
An underlying cause whose removal can help prevent the problem recurring.investigate the root cause
contributing factor
Something that helps produce an outcome without necessarily being its only cause.identify contributing factors
early-warning signal
Information showing that a problem may occur before the outcome is final.act on an early-warning signal
recurrence
The return of a problem after an earlier occurrence.prevent recurrence
measurable standard
A work requirement whose achievement can be checked against stated evidence.set a measurable standard
timeliness
Delivery within the time required for the work to be useful.improve report timeliness
rework
Work that must be done again to correct or accommodate a problem.reduce avoidable rework
support request
A specific request for help, access, or resources.make a concrete support request
action plan
A record of agreed steps, owners, and completion times.agree a focused action plan
evidence-based feedback
Feedback tied to observable events or reliable records.give evidence-based feedback

A contributing factor may be one of several causes. A root-cause claim needs stronger support than a first explanation. Coaching can address a performance gap while retaining the agreed standard and offering concrete support.

C Notice the language

Specific is clearer than severe

Feedback can be direct without making a claim about character. Name the two reports and their effect; then ask what happened. "You lack commitment" guesses at motivation. "The last two reports arrived after our agreed deadline" names an observable pattern. The Center for Creative Leadership's Situation-Behavior-Impact approach informs this distinction [2]. Once a cause is explained, a manager can offer support without quietly removing the deadline.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the occasion
For each of the last two weekly reports, we agreed on Thursday noon.
Describe the behavior
Both reports arrived on Friday morning.
Explain impact
Planning had to defer its staffing update while it waited for the figures.
Ask without assigning motive
What prevented the Thursday delivery?
Separate cause from standard
That explains the delay; we still need an early warning when the input is missing.
Offer concrete support
I will speak with the source-team manager about the handoff.
Set a trigger
If the file is missing by Wednesday noon, notify me that day.
Define follow-up
We will review the next two reports at a short Friday check-in.
Check the standard
What deadline did you understand us to have agreed?
Separate two problems
We need to address both the late source file and the missing early warning.
Limit a conclusion
These two reports show a delivery problem; they do not establish your motivation.
Explore contributing factors
Apart from the source file, which steps affected the delivery time?
Define useful support
Would an earlier handoff, access to the source owner, or a clearer trigger help most?
Make improvement measurable
For the next two reports, we will check the delivery time and whether a warning was sent.
Recognize a correction
You flagged the missing file before noon this time; that gave planning time to respond.
Agree a next step
Please confirm the action you own and when you will report its status.
6 language and precision notes
Past evidence, present expectation
Use past tense for the two arrivals. Use present or future wording for the agreed standard and next steps.
Avoid always and never
Two observed events support "the last two reports," not "you always miss deadlines." Keep the claim as narrow as the evidence.
Question before conclusion
"What prevented delivery?" seeks a cause. "Why were you careless?" builds an unverified judgment into the question.
State the time window
"The last two reports were late" identifies the evidence. "Your reports are late" can sound like a broader claim about the person's normal performance.
Bound the support question
"Would an earlier handoff or access to the source owner help?" offers practical options. Follow the answer with a named action and owner.
Recognize the observable improvement
"You warned planning before noon" names a useful action. "Your attitude is better" substitutes a personal judgment for that evidence.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is specific and evidence-based?

Answer and explanation

D. The last two reports arrived Friday, after Thursday's noon deadline.

Naming the two Friday arrivals and Thursday deadline identifies the observed events and agreed standard. The alternatives assert an unestablished cause, motivation, or broader pattern.

2. Which question does not assume fault or motive?

Answer and explanation

B. What prevented the Thursday delivery?

The question leaves the cause open for an evidence-based explanation.

3. Choose the early-warning trigger.

Answer and explanation

C. If the source file is missing Wednesday noon, notify me that day.

It specifies both a condition and a time for action.

4. Which statement preserves the expectation while offering support?

Answer and explanation

A. I will address the handoff; you still need to flag missing input early.

Support for the dependency does not remove the analyst's communication responsibility.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

Elena: International department manager / Diego: US-based analyst

Answers and explanations
  1. impact Turn 2. The delayed staffing update is the operational effect of the late reports.
  2. upstream input Turn 4. The source file is required from an earlier part of the workflow.
  3. lead time Turn 6. An earlier warning would create time for Elena to help before the deadline.
  4. flagged Turn 8. Flagging identifies the missing input and its risk without pretending to have solved it.
  5. bottleneck Turn 10. The late source-file handoff limits the speed of the downstream report.
  6. corrective Turn 12. The adjective completes "corrective action." Both steps address a known problem: the late handoff and the missing warning.
  7. performance expectation Turn 14. Elena states the deadline, warning trigger, and accuracy requirement explicitly.
  8. check-in Turn 16. A brief scheduled review is a check-in, not a new approval process.
  9. observable behavior Turn 18. Arrival times and warning messages can be observed; an attitude label guesses at motive.
  10. follow-through Turn 20. The participants must now carry out the actions they agreed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: I want to discuss the last two weekly reports. We agreed on Thursday noon, and both arrived Friday morning. Planning had to defer its staffing update while it waited.
  2. Diego: I understand the impact. I knew the reports were late, but I did not realize the staffing update had been held back.

  7. Elena: Thank you for explaining. The late file explains the production delay. It does not remove the need to warn us when the deadline is at risk.
  8. Diego: Agreed. I should have flagged the missing input on Wednesday instead of waiting for a complete answer. An update could have stated what was missing.

  11. Elena: Yes. I will speak with their manager and agree a handoff time. I will not promise a new source-file deadline on their behalf before that conversation.
  12. Diego: Then our corrective action has two parts: an agreed source handoff and an early-warning message from me if the input is missing.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two late reports without a guessed cause

The lesson's manager gives feedback on two reports that arrived one day late; the cause remains unknown.

  1. Branch manager: The last two weekly reports arrived one day after our agreed deadline.
  2. Reporting analyst: Are you saying that I haven't been taking the reporting work seriously?
  3. Branch manager: No. I'm describing the timing, not your attitude. Other teams depend on those figures.
  4. Reporting analyst: I appreciate that distinction. What would you like to establish in this conversation?
  5. Branch manager: First, what is making delivery difficult? I don't yet know the reason.
  6. Reporting analyst: I'd like to review the reporting steps before giving you an explanation I can't support.
  7. Branch manager: That's reasonable. Let's identify the obstacle before choosing a support plan.
  8. Reporting analyst: Could we also confirm the expectation for flagging a likely delay?
  9. Branch manager: Yes. We'll agree when to raise that warning and review how the next report goes.
  10. Reporting analyst: Then we have a concrete improvement to discuss without assuming the cause today.
An update without a next action

A separate fictional branch: two project updates listed problems but omitted the requested next-action section.

  1. Project manager: Your last two updates listed the problems but left out the agreed next-action section.
  2. Project coordinator: I thought describing each problem would tell readers what needed to happen.
  3. Project manager: The effect was that readers couldn't tell who was being asked to act.
  4. Project coordinator: Could you give me feedback on the distinction rather than just the format?
  5. Project manager: Certainly. A problem describes the situation; an action request identifies what someone should do next.
  6. Project coordinator: So I shouldn't invent an owner when we haven't agreed one?
  7. Project manager: Correct. Say the owner is unresolved and request a decision about it.
  8. Project coordinator: I'll use that approach in the next update and keep unknown assignments visible.
  9. Project manager: Let's review one example together as a support plan before the next distribution.
  10. Project coordinator: That helps. I understand the expectation now: actionable information, not merely an extra heading.
Feedback on rushed explanations

A separate fictional onboarding session: a trainer changed screens twice before a new coordinator finished a question.

  1. Learning manager: During today's demonstration, you changed screens twice before the coordinator finished asking a question.
  2. Trainer: I was trying to complete the agenda. Did the coordinator say the explanation was unclear?
  3. Learning manager: I haven't asked them, so I can't claim that. My feedback concerns the observed interruptions.
  4. Trainer: What change would make the next demonstration more useful without abandoning the schedule?
  5. Learning manager: Could you pause for the question and state what you are about to show?
  6. Trainer: That may take longer. Can we review the number of examples in the session?
  7. Learning manager: Yes. Our support plan can include a smaller agenda with time for clarification.
  8. Trainer: I'd also like to ask the coordinator which step they wanted explained.
  9. Learning manager: Good. That invites their perspective instead of assuming the effect on their learning.
  10. Trainer: I'll do that and try the revised pacing at the next session.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two late reports and one missing handoff
  1. Read turns 1-8 and underline the two observed events and their impact. Compare your markings with the facts on the briefing page.
  2. Switch roles for turns 9-20. Read the Wednesday trigger and Thursday deadline exactly as printed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A missed status update

An engineer missed two agreed Tuesday updates. The manager knows the dates but does not know the reason. The next update is still due Tuesday at 2 p.m.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The updates were missing on the last two Tuesdays. What ___ sending them?"

Answer and explanation

A. prevented

The question asks for a cause without assuming a motive.

2. Engineer: "I waited for a full answer. Next time I will ___ the unresolved item."

Answer and explanation

C. flag

A status update can identify what is unresolved rather than wait for completion.

3. Manager: "The next update ___ Tuesday at 2 p.m."

Answer and explanation

D. remains due

The conversation has not changed the agreed deadline.

4. Engineer: "Understood. I will report what is confirmed and what is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

"Pending" preserves the distinction between completed and unresolved work.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Give specific, useful feedback
Original fictional case: The weekly report is due at noon on Thursday. In each of the last two weeks it arrived on Friday morning. Planning had to defer a staffing update. Elena does not know why the reports were late. Diego depends on a source file from another team. This is a coaching discussion, not a fictional disciplinary procedure.
Language workshop: Two late reports and one missing handoff
Communication goal: Describe behavior, explain operational impact, ask about causes, and agree a measurable next step.
Language guidance: An impact is an effect you can describe; an attribution is an explanation you assign. Check the explanation before treating it as a fact. A check-in reviews progress; follow-through means carrying out the agreed action.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): For each of the last two weekly reports, we agreed on Thursday noon. / Both reports arrived on Friday morning. / Planning had to defer its staffing update while it waited for the figures. / What prevented the Thursday delivery? / That explains the delay; we still need an early warning when the input is missing. / I will speak with the source-team manager about the handoff. / If the file is missing by Wednesday noon, notify me that day. / We will review the next two reports at a short Friday check-in. / What deadline did you understand us to have agreed? / We need to address both the late source file and the missing early warning. / These two reports show a delivery problem; they do not establish your motivation. / Apart from the source file, which steps affected the delivery time? / Would an earlier handoff, access to the source owner, or a clearer trigger help most? / For the next two reports, we will check the delivery time and whether a warning was sent. / You flagged the missing file before noon this time; that gave planning time to respond. / Please confirm the action you own and when you will report its status.
Vocabulary: observable behavior: An action that can be described without guessing at motive. / impact: The effect of an action on people or work. / attribution: An explanation assigned to someone's behavior or an event. / pattern: A repeated feature across more than one event. / bottleneck: A point that limits the flow or speed of work. / upstream input: Information or work required from an earlier stage or another team. / lead time: The interval needed before an output or action can be ready. / corrective action: An agreed step to address a problem or prevent its recurrence. / check-in: A brief conversation to review status or provide support. / follow-through: Carrying out what was agreed, not just agreeing to it. / performance expectation: A clear description of the work standard required. / SBI: Situation-Behavior-Impact: CCL's sequence for specific feedback. / coaching: Support that helps someone improve specific work or skills. / performance gap: The difference between an agreed standard and observed performance. / root cause: An underlying cause whose removal can help prevent the problem recurring. / contributing factor: Something that helps produce an outcome without necessarily being its only cause. / early-warning signal: Information showing that a problem may occur before the outcome is final. / recurrence: The return of a problem after an earlier occurrence. / measurable standard: A work requirement whose achievement can be checked against stated evidence. / timeliness: Delivery within the time required for the work to be useful. / rework: Work that must be done again to correct or accommodate a problem. / support request: A specific request for help, access, or resources. / action plan: A record of agreed steps, owners, and completion times. / evidence-based feedback: Feedback tied to observable events or reliable records.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Coordinate across officesOne report, two different purposes+

Your goal

Negotiate a reporting interface, translate local constraints, and define exceptions without weakening oversight.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

One report, two different purposes

Headquarters asks the branch to re-enter eighteen figures into a daily spreadsheet. Most figures are already in the shared dashboard, and re-entry takes one hour each day. Ren needs early warning of service failures, not a second archive of every figure. Both offices can agree the format and reporting cadence for this internal process. They cannot change any separate mandatory reporting requirement.

Nadia: International manager of a US branch / Ren: Headquarters operations manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does headquarters primarily need?

Answer and explanation

C. Early warning of service failures

Ren explicitly needs early warning of service failures. The other arrangements are not the stated business purpose and could delay or duplicate the information.

2. What is the known cost of daily re-entry?

Answer and explanation

A. One hour each day

The case specifies one hour per day; eighteen is the number of figures.

3. What must remain outside this negotiation?

Answer and explanation

B. Separate mandatory reporting requirements

The participants may redesign this internal process, not waive separate requirements.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An exception is an event outside a defined condition, not anything someone dislikes. A threshold triggers an action; a cadence sets its rhythm. An audit trail preserves the history rather than replacing it with the latest number.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
reporting cadence
How often a report or update is provided.agree a reporting cadence
exception reporting
Reporting deviations from agreed conditions rather than every routine item.use exception reporting
threshold
A defined point that triggers attention or action.set an alert threshold
single source of truth
The agreed authoritative record for a particular set of information.maintain a single source of truth
duplicate entry
Recording the same information again in another place.eliminate duplicate entry
audit trail
A record that shows changes, actions, or supporting evidence.preserve an audit trail
visibility
Access to information needed to understand a situation.maintain visibility
local context
Conditions specific to the branch or operating environment.explain the local context
handoff
Transfer of information or responsibility between people or teams.clarify the handoff
service level
An agreed or defined standard of service performance.monitor the service level
reporting burden
The time and effort required to prepare and maintain reports.reduce the reporting burden
escalation path
The agreed route for bringing an issue to the right authority.follow the escalation path
reporting line
The management relationship showing whom a person reports to.clarify the reporting line
data owner
The role answerable for a defined set of data and its use or maintenance.identify the data owner
reporting period
The span of time covered by a report.specify the reporting period
refresh cycle
How often information is updated in a system or report.confirm the refresh cycle
data lineage
The history of where data came from and how it was transformed.trace the data lineage
version control
A method for identifying versions and tracking changes to a record.maintain version control
data completeness
The extent to which the required information is present.check data completeness
acknowledgment time
The agreed time within which a receiver confirms receipt.set an acknowledgment time
operating model
The arrangement of roles, processes, and decision rights used to run the work.align the operating model
local discretion
The choices a branch or local team may make within shared requirements.define local discretion
cross-functional dependency
Work in one function that depends on an input or decision from another.manage a cross-functional dependency
information requirement
The specific information needed for a stated purpose.clarify the information requirement

A reporting period says which dates the figures cover; a refresh cycle says when they are updated. Data lineage explains origin and transformation; an audit trail records changes or actions. State which history is needed.

C Notice the language

Translate the business need

Headquarters may experience fewer updates as loss of visibility; a branch may experience repeated requests as distrust. Neither interpretation is established by the request alone. Ask what decision the information supports, then propose a format that serves it. Explain the local workload in measurable terms. Avoid describing one office as "bureaucratic" or the other as "uncooperative" when the real disagreement concerns frequency, evidence, and responsibility.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about use
Which decision does each daily figure support?
Quantify the burden
Re-entering the eighteen figures takes one hour each day.
Preserve the purpose
I want to reduce duplication, not reduce your visibility.
Offer a reporting design
Could we use the dashboard for routine figures and a separate alert for exceptions?
Define an exception
An exception means a missed service target or an unassigned urgent case.
Protect authority
This proposal does not change any separate mandatory report.
Agree a handoff
The branch duty lead will send the alert and name the action owner.
Test the arrangement
Let us trial the format for two weeks and review missed alerts and preparation time.
Check data freshness
As of what time are these figures current?
Distinguish reporting terms
The reporting line tells us who manages the analyst; the cadence tells us when updates arrive.
Name the source
Which record should both offices use if the spreadsheet and dashboard disagree?
Locate a change
Can we trace that revised figure to its source and see when it changed?
Set a response expectation
Please confirm receipt by the agreed acknowledgment time, even if the issue is unresolved.
Clarify local flexibility
Which parts of the format may the branch adapt without changing the shared requirement?
Define time precisely
Does the reporting period end at midnight in New York or at headquarters?
Close a cross-office gap
Let us name an owner on each side of the handoff, including cover for absences.
6 language and precision notes
Not X, but Y
"Not less visibility, but less duplicate entry" distinguishes the business goal from the proposed method.
Unless + condition
"No separate alert is needed unless the threshold is crossed" sets a condition. It does not mean that routine monitoring stops.
Define before using jargon
Explain "exception reporting" with the actual trigger. Without a trigger, people may use the same term for different practices.
As of a time
"Current as of 3 p.m. New York time" identifies the information's cutoff. It does not promise that the figure will stay unchanged.
Avoid ambiguous frequency
"Every two weeks" and "twice a week" distinguish meanings that people sometimes attach to "biweekly." State the actual days when timing matters.
Separate receipt from resolution
"We will acknowledge within one hour" promises a response confirming receipt. It does not promise that the issue will be solved within that hour.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question discovers the business purpose of the report?

Answer and explanation

B. Which decisions require these daily figures?

It asks how the data will be used without assuming a motive.

2. Which proposal preserves routine access while reducing re-entry?

Answer and explanation

C. Keep the dashboard and send defined exception alerts.

The dashboard supplies routine visibility; explicit alerts draw attention to deviations.

3. Which trigger is operationally precise?

Answer and explanation

D. When a service target is missed or an urgent case has no owner

It identifies observable conditions rather than a subjective mood.

4. Choose the correct meaning of "unless" here: "Send an alert unless the issue is already logged with an owner."

Answer and explanation

A. The logged-and-owned condition is an exception to sending a new alert.

"Unless" introduces the condition under which the stated action is not required.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

One report, two different purposes

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nadia: International manager of a US branch / Ren: Headquarters operations manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • handoff
  • reporting burden
  • threshold
  • cadence
  • waiver
  • audit trail
  • duplicate entry
  • escalation path
  • single source of truth
  • visibility
  1. Ren
    We need the eighteen branch figures in the daily spreadsheet. When they arrive late, headquarters has less confidence that it can spot a service problem quickly.
  2. Nadia
    I understand the need for 1. May I ask which decision each figure supports? Most of them are already in the shared dashboard.
  3. Ren
    The urgent need is to see a missed service target before it becomes a larger problem. The spreadsheet grew over time because different people requested extra columns.
  4. Nadia
    That explains the purpose. The current 2 takes one hour every day. I would like to remove the repeated work without hiding any figures.
  5. Ren
    If we stop receiving the spreadsheet, how will we know whether the dashboard has changed? We cannot watch every branch screen continuously.
  6. Nadia
    We could keep the dashboard as the 3 and add a short alert when an agreed condition changes. The alert would link to the actual record.
  7. Ren
    What would trigger an alert? I do not want different branches applying different definitions of an exception.
  8. Nadia
    Then let us define the 4: a missed service target or an urgent case without an assigned owner. Those are the events this internal alert will cover.
  9. Ren
    Who will send the alert? I want to avoid a situation in which the analyst assumes the manager has done it and the manager assumes the opposite.
  10. Nadia
    The branch duty lead will own the 5. The alert will identify the issue, its current owner, and the next update time. A deputy will cover absence.
  11. Ren
    What if the figures change afterward? I still need to see what we knew when the first alert came in.
  12. Nadia
    We will preserve an 6 in the shared record. The alert will not replace the underlying data or erase earlier values.
  13. Ren
    All right. Check for exceptions daily and summarize weekly. That would save me reading the same eighteen figures every morning, too.
  14. Nadia
    Let us record that 7 explicitly: monitor daily, alert on the defined events, and summarize weekly. A quiet day does not mean monitoring has stopped.
  15. Ren
    Agreed, but some reports serve other requirements. I cannot authorize the branch to stop those as part of this conversation.
  16. Nadia
    Nor am I asking you to. This is a change to an internal reporting process, not a 8 of any separate mandatory requirement.
  17. Ren
    Let us trial the arrangement for two weeks. We should compare the time spent preparing updates and check whether any relevant alert was missed.
  18. Nadia
    I will record those as the trial criteria. The aim is a lower 9 with no loss of the agreed warning function.
  19. Ren
    If an alert has no response, send it to me and the duty manager instead of copying everyone. I will make sure the receiving side knows its responsibility.
  20. Nadia
    Then we have an 10 as well as a reporting format. I will send the one-page agreement, and we will confirm the named owners before the trial starts.

Answers and explanations
  1. visibility Turn 2. Headquarters needs access to information about branch service performance.
  2. duplicate entry Turn 4. The figures are being manually recorded again even though they are already available.
  3. single source of truth Turn 6. The shared dashboard remains the agreed authoritative record, rather than competing spreadsheets.
  4. threshold Turn 8. The missed target or unassigned urgent case defines when an alert is required.
  5. handoff Turn 10. The duty lead transfers the information with an owner and an update time.
  6. audit trail Turn 12. A history of data and changes lets the offices reconstruct what was known.
  7. cadence Turn 14. Daily monitoring, event-triggered alerts, and weekly summaries define the rhythm of reporting.
  8. waiver Turn 16. A waiver would remove a requirement; this internal redesign does not do that.
  9. reporting burden Turn 18. The trial aims to reduce preparation time while preserving useful information.
  10. escalation path Turn 20. Ren names the route to use when an alert has not received a response.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ren: We need the eighteen branch figures in the daily spreadsheet. When they arrive late, headquarters has less confidence that it can spot a service problem quickly.
  2. Nadia: I understand the need for visibility. May I ask which decision each figure supports? Most of them are already in the shared dashboard.
  3. Ren: The urgent need is to see a missed service target before it becomes a larger problem. The spreadsheet grew over time because different people requested extra columns.
  4. Nadia: That explains the purpose. The current duplicate entry takes one hour every day. I would like to remove the repeated work without hiding any figures.
  5. Ren: If we stop receiving the spreadsheet, how will we know whether the dashboard has changed? We cannot watch every branch screen continuously.
  6. Nadia: We could keep the dashboard as the single source of truth and add a short alert when an agreed condition changes. The alert would link to the actual record.
  7. Ren: What would trigger an alert? I do not want different branches applying different definitions of an exception.
  8. Nadia: Then let us define the threshold: a missed service target or an urgent case without an assigned owner. Those are the events this internal alert will cover.
  9. Ren: Who will send the alert? I want to avoid a situation in which the analyst assumes the manager has done it and the manager assumes the opposite.
  10. Nadia: The branch duty lead will own the handoff. The alert will identify the issue, its current owner, and the next update time. A deputy will cover absence.
  11. Ren: What if the figures change afterward? I still need to see what we knew when the first alert came in.
  12. Nadia: We will preserve an audit trail in the shared record. The alert will not replace the underlying data or erase earlier values.
  13. Ren: All right. Check for exceptions daily and summarize weekly. That would save me reading the same eighteen figures every morning, too.
  14. Nadia: Let us record that cadence explicitly: monitor daily, alert on the defined events, and summarize weekly. A quiet day does not mean monitoring has stopped.
  15. Ren: Agreed, but some reports serve other requirements. I cannot authorize the branch to stop those as part of this conversation.
  16. Nadia: Nor am I asking you to. This is a change to an internal reporting process, not a waiver of any separate mandatory requirement.
  17. Ren: Let us trial the arrangement for two weeks. We should compare the time spent preparing updates and check whether any relevant alert was missed.
  18. Nadia: I will record those as the trial criteria. The aim is a lower reporting burden with no loss of the agreed warning function.
  19. Ren: If an alert has no response, send it to me and the duty manager instead of copying everyone. I will make sure the receiving side knows its responsibility.
  20. Nadia: Then we have an escalation path as well as a reporting format. I will send the one-page agreement, and we will confirm the named owners before the trial starts.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Detail versus decision information

Headquarters and the branch discuss the lesson's daily report, which takes the branch an hour to prepare.

  1. Headquarters coordinator: We ask for daily detail, but I hear the report takes your branch an hour.
  2. Branch manager: Yes. Could we distinguish information you need to act from information that's merely available?
  3. Headquarters coordinator: That's useful. We haven't explained which decisions the daily detail supports.
  4. Branch manager: Without that explanation, we can't judge which parts of the workload add value.
  5. Headquarters coordinator: Would an operating agreement simply mean another reporting template for your team?
  6. Branch manager: Here, it would mean agreeing what we share, why, how often, and who responds.
  7. Headquarters coordinator: So the template would follow the information need, rather than define it?
  8. Branch manager: Exactly. Stakeholder engagement starts with asking users of the report what they actually require.
  9. Headquarters coordinator: I'll bring those decision needs to a discussion before requesting further detail.
  10. Branch manager: Then we can assess the workload and negotiate a reporting rhythm on that basis.
An exception report is not a complete log

A separate fictional branch proposes reporting delayed deliveries only; headquarters currently receives a log of every delivery.

  1. Branch analyst: Could we discuss an exception report instead of sending the full delivery log each day?
  2. Regional planner: Does exception report mean you would stop recording deliveries that arrive on time?
  3. Branch analyst: No. We would retain the complete log but highlight delays in the shared update.
  4. Regional planner: Then the distinction is between what you record and what you routinely send us?
  5. Branch analyst: Correct. The operating agreement would need to define which exceptions require attention.
  6. Regional planner: I sometimes need the full log for planning. Would that remain accessible?
  7. Branch analyst: That access needs agreement. I'm not proposing to remove it without discussing your needs.
  8. Regional planner: Let's identify when I need full detail and when a delay summary is sufficient.
  9. Branch analyst: We can then estimate the workload instead of assuming the shorter report saves time.
  10. Regional planner: Good. Bring both information needs into the proposal, including how I request the full log.
Consulted is not copied

A separate fictional branch project copies the service desk on announcements but has not asked about implementation workload.

  1. Project sponsor: The service desk receives every announcement, so stakeholder engagement is already covered.
  2. Branch operations lead: Receiving information isn't the same as being consulted about the work it creates.
  3. Project sponsor: What would consultation add beyond another copy of the project update?
  4. Branch operations lead: It would let the service desk explain its workload and flag unresolved support needs.
  5. Project sponsor: Would that give the desk authority to approve every project change?
  6. Branch operations lead: Not automatically. Input and decision rights are separate parts of an operating agreement.
  7. Project sponsor: So we should ask for their perspective without promising them approval authority?
  8. Branch operations lead: Yes, and explain how their input will be considered in the decision.
  9. Project sponsor: I'll invite a discussion about implementation work before calling the engagement complete.
  10. Branch operations lead: That will give us evidence about support needs instead of treating email distribution as participation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

One report, two different purposes
  1. Read the dialogue with Nadia and Ren as equal partners in designing this internal process, not as national stereotypes.
  2. Switch roles for turns 7-16. Read the alert threshold, cadence, and limits without dropping any condition.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A repeated staffing spreadsheet

A shared roster already lists confirmed shifts. The office needs a separate alert only when a shift remains unfilled at the agreed checkpoint. Employment and safety requirements remain unchanged.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Ren: "Keep the roster as the authoritative ___."

Answer and explanation

A. record

The roster is the agreed record of confirmed shifts.

2. Nadia: "At the checkpoint, an unfilled shift triggers an ___."

Answer and explanation

D. alert

The defined exception needs an alert, not assumed approval.

3. Ren: "This change reduces ___, not required safeguards."

Answer and explanation

B. duplication

The aim is less repeated administration while retaining the safeguards.

4. Nadia: "We will name an owner and a deputy for the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. handoff

Naming a sender and backup makes the information transfer reliable.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Cross-Cultural Leadership English
Professional audience: managers, cross-border teams, HR partners, and meeting facilitators
Scope: Ask about individual preferences and team norms. Nationality, accent, silence, or directness does not tell you what a person believes or how they will behave.
Lesson 07: Coordinate across offices
Original fictional case: Headquarters asks the branch to re-enter eighteen figures into a daily spreadsheet. Most figures are already in the shared dashboard, and re-entry takes one hour each day. Ren needs early warning of service failures, not a second archive of every figure. Both offices can agree the format and reporting cadence for this internal process. They cannot change any separate mandatory reporting requirement.
Language workshop: One report, two different purposes
Communication goal: Negotiate a reporting interface, translate local constraints, and define exceptions without weakening oversight.
Language guidance: An exception is an event outside a defined condition, not anything someone dislikes. A threshold triggers an action; a cadence sets its rhythm. An audit trail preserves the history rather than replacing it with the latest number.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which decision does each daily figure support? / Re-entering the eighteen figures takes one hour each day. / I want to reduce duplication, not reduce your visibility. / Could we use the dashboard for routine figures and a separate alert for exceptions? / An exception means a missed service target or an unassigned urgent case. / This proposal does not change any separate mandatory report. / The branch duty lead will send the alert and name the action owner. / Let us trial the format for two weeks and review missed alerts and preparation time. / As of what time are these figures current? / The reporting line tells us who manages the analyst; the cadence tells us when updates arrive. / Which record should both offices use if the spreadsheet and dashboard disagree? / Can we trace that revised figure to its source and see when it changed? / Please confirm receipt by the agreed acknowledgment time, even if the issue is unresolved. / Which parts of the format may the branch adapt without changing the shared requirement? / Does the reporting period end at midnight in New York or at headquarters? / Let us name an owner on each side of the handoff, including cover for absences.
Vocabulary: reporting cadence: How often a report or update is provided. / exception reporting: Reporting deviations from agreed conditions rather than every routine item. / threshold: A defined point that triggers attention or action. / single source of truth: The agreed authoritative record for a particular set of information. / duplicate entry: Recording the same information again in another place. / audit trail: A record that shows changes, actions, or supporting evidence. / visibility: Access to information needed to understand a situation. / local context: Conditions specific to the branch or operating environment. / handoff: Transfer of information or responsibility between people or teams. / service level: An agreed or defined standard of service performance. / reporting burden: The time and effort required to prepare and maintain reports. / escalation path: The agreed route for bringing an issue to the right authority. / reporting line: The management relationship showing whom a person reports to. / data owner: The role answerable for a defined set of data and its use or maintenance. / reporting period: The span of time covered by a report. / refresh cycle: How often information is updated in a system or report. / data lineage: The history of where data came from and how it was transformed. / version control: A method for identifying versions and tracking changes to a record. / data completeness: The extent to which the required information is present. / acknowledgment time: The agreed time within which a receiver confirms receipt. / operating model: The arrangement of roles, processes, and decision rights used to run the work. / local discretion: The choices a branch or local team may make within shared requirements. / cross-functional dependency: Work in one function that depends on an input or decision from another. / information requirement: The specific information needed for a stated purpose.
Speaking task: Read the dialogue with Nadia and Ren as equal partners in designing this internal process, not as national stereotypes. Switch roles for turns 7-16. Read the alert threshold, cadence, and limits without dropping any condition. Complete and read the staffing exchange. End by repeating exactly what the new arrangement does not change.
Partner: Nadia: International manager of a US branch / Ren: Headquarters operations manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A shared roster already lists confirmed shifts. The office needs a separate alert only when a shift remains unfilled at the agreed checkpoint. Employment and safety requirements remain unchanged.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ren: Let us trial the arrangement for two weeks. We should compare the time spent preparing updates and check whether any relevant alert was missed.
Nadia: I will record those as the trial criteria. The aim is a lower reporting burden with no loss of the agreed warning function.
Ren: If an alert has no response, send it to me and the duty manager instead of copying everyone. I will make sure the receiving side knows its responsibility.
Nadia: Then we have an escalation path as well as a reporting format. I will send the one-page agreement, and we will confirm the named owners before the trial starts.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: reporting cadence: How often a report or update is provided. / exception reporting: Reporting deviations from agreed conditions rather than every routine item. / threshold: A defined point that triggers attention or action. / single source of truth: The agreed authoritative record for a particular set of information. / duplicate entry: Recording the same information again in another place. / audit trail: A record that shows changes, actions, or supporting evidence. / visibility: Access to information needed to understand a situation. / local context: Conditions specific to the branch or operating environment. / handoff: Transfer of information or responsibility between people or teams. / service level: An agreed or defined standard of service performance. / reporting burden: The time and effort required to prepare and maintain reports. / escalation path: The agreed route for bringing an issue to the right authority. / reporting line: The management relationship showing whom a person reports to. / data owner: The role answerable for a defined set of data and its use or maintenance. / reporting period: The span of time covered by a report. / refresh cycle: How often information is updated in a system or report. / data lineage: The history of where data came from and how it was transformed. / version control: A method for identifying versions and tracking changes to a record. / data completeness: The extent to which the required information is present. / acknowledgment time: The agreed time within which a receiver confirms receipt. / operating model: The arrangement of roles, processes, and decision rights used to run the work. / local discretion: The choices a branch or local team may make within shared requirements. / cross-functional dependency: Work in one function that depends on an input or decision from another. / information requirement: The specific information needed for a stated purpose.
Speaking task: Read the dialogue with Nadia and Ren as equal partners in designing this internal process, not as national stereotypes. Switch roles for turns 7-16. Read the alert threshold, cadence, and limits without dropping any condition. Complete and read the staffing exchange. End by repeating exactly what the new arrangement does not change.
Partner: Nadia: International manager of a US branch / Ren: Headquarters operations manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A shared roster already lists confirmed shifts. The office needs a separate alert only when a shift remains unfilled at the agreed checkpoint. Employment and safety requirements remain unchanged.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ren: Let us trial the arrangement for two weeks. We should compare the time spent preparing updates and check whether any relevant alert was missed.
Nadia: I will record those as the trial criteria. The aim is a lower reporting burden with no loss of the agreed warning function.
Ren: If an alert has no response, send it to me and the duty manager instead of copying everyone. I will make sure the receiving side knows its responsibility.
Nadia: Then we have an escalation path as well as a reporting format. I will send the one-page agreement, and we will confirm the named owners before the trial starts.
END REFERENCE
08Repair misunderstandings and build trustAn apology that changes the next meeting+

Your goal

Acknowledge a specific impact, separate intent from effect, repair the record, and agree a verifiable change.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

An apology that changes the next meeting

Daniel interrupted Lena twice while she explained a customer risk. He then summarized her concern as a timing preference, which was inaccurate. Lena later said she felt dismissed. The risk itself remains unresolved. Daniel wants to repair the interaction and correct the meeting record without making an empty promise that the project decision will go Lena's way.

Daniel: International project manager / Lena: US-based risk analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What must Daniel correct in the record?

Answer and explanation

B. The inaccurate description of Lena's concern

He described a customer risk as a timing preference.

2. What can a sincere repair legitimately promise?

Answer and explanation

C. A corrected record and a protected turn to explain the risk.

Daniel can change the process and record without promising a business outcome.

3. What remains open after an apology?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual customer risk

Repairing the interaction does not itself resolve the substantive risk.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An explanation describes why something happened; it does not automatically excuse the impact. A correction replaces inaccurate information, while a retraction withdraws a statement. Follow-up checks progress; follow-through carries out the promise.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
acknowledge
Recognize an action, fact, or experience explicitly.acknowledge the impact
intent
What someone meant or hoped to accomplish.explain an intent
impact
What an action actually did or how it affected someone.separate intent from impact
defensiveness
Responding mainly to protect oneself from criticism.reduce defensiveness
repair
An action that addresses a communication or relationship failure.make a concrete repair
retraction
Withdrawal of a statement that should no longer stand.issue a retraction
correction
A change that makes a record or statement accurate.circulate a correction
mischaracterize
Describe someone's position inaccurately or unfairly.mischaracterize a concern
good faith
An honest effort to engage rather than mislead or obstruct.act in good faith
commitment
An undertaking to do something specific.make a limited commitment
follow-up
Later action to check or continue an agreed step.set a follow-up
working agreement
Explicit expectations for how people will cooperate.establish a working agreement
ownership
Acceptance that an action or problem is yours to address.take ownership of the mistake
omission
Something important left out of a statement or record.correct an omission
attribution of credit
Identification of the person whose contribution is being recognized.ensure accurate attribution of credit
intent-impact gap
The difference between an intended effect and what actually happened.address the intent-impact gap
de-escalation
Reducing the intensity of a conflict without necessarily resolving the issue.support de-escalation
face-saving
Allowing someone to preserve dignity while a mistake is addressed.offer a face-saving correction
reciprocity
A pattern of responding to another person's constructive action with one of your own.build reciprocity over time
remedy
An action intended to put a specific problem right.agree a proportionate remedy
corroboration
Additional evidence that supports an account of what happened.seek corroboration of the account
non-defensive response
A reply that engages with criticism without immediately protecting or excusing oneself.give a non-defensive response
reputational harm
Damage to how others judge a person's competence or reliability.address reputational harm
procedural fairness
Fairness in how views are heard and a decision is reached.protect procedural fairness

De-escalation reduces tension; it does not correct an inaccurate record by itself. A remedy names what will put the problem right. Procedural fairness concerns the hearing and decision process, not a guaranteed preferred outcome.

C Notice the language

An explanation is not an eraser

"I was trying to keep us on time" can explain an intention, but it does not undo an interruption or an inaccurate record. Start with what you did and the effect reported by the other person. Then state a concrete repair. The person receiving the apology does not owe instant reassurance. Disagreement about the project may remain after the relationship improves. A useful repair changes the process without promising a predetermined business decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Own the action
I interrupted you twice and summarized your concern inaccurately.
Apologize directly
I am sorry I cut you off.
Acknowledge the effect
You did not get a fair chance to explain the customer risk.
Explain without excusing
I was trying to manage time; that does not justify misrepresenting your point.
Correct the record
I will send a correction to everyone who received the original summary.
Make a process commitment
You will have an uninterrupted turn before we decide.
Avoid a false outcome promise
I cannot promise agreement, but I can promise an accurate review.
Check the repair
After the next meeting, we will check whether the new turn-taking rule was followed.
Own an omission
I left your contribution out of the summary, and I will correct that.
Restore credit
The revised note will name your contribution and link to the original proposal.
Invite a factual correction
Which part of my account is still inaccurate?
Avoid requesting reassurance
You do not need to tell me that everything is fine today.
Preserve dignity and accuracy
We can correct the record without making another personal judgment.
Acknowledge remaining disagreement
Correcting my account does not require us to agree on the recommendation.
Set a verifiable remedy
I will send the correction to the original recipients by this afternoon.
Allow trust to develop
I understand that trust will depend on what I do in the next meeting.
6 language and precision notes
Own the subject
"I interrupted you" names the actor. "Interruptions happened" removes that responsibility from the sentence.
Avoid conditional apologies
"I am sorry if you felt dismissed" makes the reported impact sound doubtful. "I am sorry I cut you off" owns a specific action.
Process versus outcome
"We will review the risk accurately" is a process commitment. "Your recommendation will be approved" promises a result before the review.
Use a concrete apology
"I am sorry I omitted your contribution" names the action. "I am sorry about everything" leaves the specific error unclear.
Make the remedy visible
"I will make it right" is broad. "I will name your contribution in a correction to the original recipients" specifies an action others can check.
Acknowledge without promising agreement
"I understand the risk you raised" recognizes the concern. "I accept your recommendation" adopts the proposed response. Keep those meanings separate.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Choose the apology that owns a specific action.

Answer and explanation

D. I am sorry I interrupted you twice.

The speaker accepts responsibility for an observable action without shifting blame.

2. Which sentence explains intent without using it as an excuse?

Answer and explanation

B. I wanted to save time; I still should not have cut you off.

The explanation does not remove responsibility for the interruption.

3. Which commitment is within Daniel's control?

Answer and explanation

C. I will circulate the correction to the original recipients.

Daniel can circulate his correction. Trust, elimination of the risk, and the eventual business decision are not guaranteed by that action.

4. What does restoring trust require here beyond an apology?

Answer and explanation

A. An accurate record and observable follow-through

The repair is demonstrated by actions; it does not require instant forgiveness or agreement.

An apology that changes the next meeting

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Daniel: International project manager / Lena: US-based risk analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.
